\chapter{Control Flow}
EDADSL provides several control flow constructs for conditional execution, iteration, and program flow control.

\section{Conditional Execution}
Conditional execution uses the \texttt{if} keyword followed by a boolean expression and a code block. Additional conditions can be tested with \texttt{otherwise}. A final \texttt{else} clause provides a fallback code block executed when no conditions match:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
conditional = "if", boolean_expression, 
              "::", code_block,
              { "otherwise", boolean_expression, 
                "::", code_block },
              ["else", "::", code_block]

Example:
$flag [bool]= True
if $flag ::{
    f.util.print("Flag is set")
} otherwise $other ::{
    f.util.print("Other condition")
} else ::{
    f.util.print("Flag is not set")
}
\end{verbatim}

Basic \texttt{if} without \texttt{else}:
\begin{verbatim}
Example:
$flag [bool]= True
if $flag ::{
    f.util.print("Flag is set")
}
\end{verbatim}

\section{Cases}
The \texttt{cases} statement provides multi-branch conditional execution with a bound variable. It evaluates a list of boolean expressions in order and executes the first matching branch. This is a cleaner alternative to long \texttt{if/otherwise} chains when multiple conditions need to be tested against a common value.

\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
cases = "cases", "(", variable, typed_assign, data, ")", 
        "::", "{", { case_clause }, [ otherwise_clause ], "}"

case_clause = "case", "(", boolean_expression, ")", 
              "::", code_block

otherwise_clause = "otherwise", "::", code_block
\end{verbatim}

\begin{itemize}
\item The expression in the \texttt{cases(...)} header is evaluated once and bound to the variable
\item Each \texttt{case} clause contains a boolean expression that can reference the bound variable
\item Case clauses are evaluated in order; the first \texttt{True} wins
\item The optional \texttt{otherwise} clause is a fallback when no case matches
\item If no case matches and no \texttt{otherwise} is present, execution continues silently
\item The bound variable is block-scoped to the \texttt{cases} block
\end{itemize}

Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
# Basic usage
$r [real]= f.tnsr.norm($v)
cases ($r [real]= f.tnsr.norm($v)) ::{
    case ($r < 0.1) ::{
        f.util.print("small")
    } case ($r < 1.0) ::{
        f.util.print("medium")
    } otherwise ::{
        f.util.print("large")
    }
}

# Without otherwise (silently continues if no match)
cases ($x [int]= f.rng.int(1, 10)) ::{
    case ($x == 1) ::{
        f.util.print("got one")
    } case ($x == 2) ::{
        f.util.print("got two")
    }
}

# Boolean expression doesn't need to reference bound variable
$mode [string]= "fast"
cases ($mode [string]= "fast") ::{
    case ($mode == "slow") ::{
        f.util.print("slow mode")
    } case ($mode == "fast") ::{
        f.util.print("fast mode")
    } otherwise ::{
        f.util.print("unknown mode")
    }
}
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Equivalent if/otherwise chain}
The \texttt{cases} statement is equivalent to an \texttt{if/otherwise} chain:
\begin{verbatim}
# cases statement:
cases ($r [real]= f.tnsr.norm($v)) ::{
    case ($r < 0.1) ::{
        f.util.print("small")
    } case ($r < 1.0) ::{
        f.util.print("medium")
    } otherwise ::{
        f.util.print("large")
    }
}

# Equivalent if/otherwise:
$r [real]= f.tnsr.norm($v)
if $r < 0.1 ::{
    f.util.print("small")
} otherwise $r < 1.0 ::{
    f.util.print("medium")
} else ::{
    f.util.print("large")
}
\end{verbatim}

\section{Alias Statements}
Alias statements create short names for built-in functions or namespaces. Aliases are file-scoped and cannot shadow existing variables or aliases.

\subsection{Function Alias}
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
alias_stmt = "alias", function_ref, "as", id

Example:
alias f.trig.sin as sin
alias f.trig.cos as cos
$y = sin($x)
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Namespace Alias}
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
alias_stmt = "alias", namespace, "as", id

Example:
alias f.trig as trig
$y = trig.sin($x)
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Edge Cases}
\begin{itemize}
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the alias name conflicts with an existing variable, alias, or built-in name.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the alias target does not exist (e.g., \texttt{alias f.trig.nonexistent as x}).
\item Aliases are file-scoped: they only apply within the current file.
\item Aliases cannot be reassigned after declaration.
\end{itemize}

\section{For Loops}
For loops iterate over a list or set:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
iterator = "for", variable, "E", 
           (list | set), "::", code_block

Example:
$colors [list]= ["red", "green", "blue"]
for $c E $colors ::{
    f.util.print($c)
}
\end{verbatim}
The loop variable (\texttt{\$c}) takes on each value in the list/set in sequence.

\paragraph{Variable Scoping}
The loop variable is block-scoped: it is only visible inside the loop body and is destroyed when the loop exits. If the loop iterates over an empty list, the loop body never executes and the variable is never initialized; accessing it after the loop raises a \texttt{Runtime Error}.

\section{While Loops}
While loops repeat a code block as long as a boolean condition evaluates to \texttt{True}.

\subsection{Basic While Loop}
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
while_loop = "while", boolean_expression, 
             "::", code_block

Example:
$i [int]= 0
while $i < 5 ::{
    f.util.print($i)
    $i = $i + 1
}
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Variable Scoping}
While loops do not introduce any implicit variables. Any variables declared inside the loop body are block-scoped and destroyed when the loop exits. Variables used in the loop condition must be declared before the loop.

\subsection{While with Else Clause}
An optional \texttt{else} clause executes when the loop condition becomes \texttt{False}. If the loop exits via \texttt{break}, the \texttt{else} clause is \textbf{not} executed:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
while_loop = "while", boolean_expression, 
             "::", code_block,
             ["else", "::", code_block]

Example:
$i [int]= 0
while $i < 5 ::{
    $i = $i + 1
} else ::{
    f.util.print("Loop completed normally")
}

Example with break:
$i [int]= 0
while $i < 10 ::{
    if $i == 3 ::{
        break
    }
    $i = $i + 1
} else ::{
    f.util.print("This will NOT be printed")
}
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Do-While Loops}
Do-while loops execute the code block \textbf{at least once} before evaluating the condition:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
do_while = "do", "::", code_block,
           "while", boolean_expression

Example:
$count [int]= 0
do ::{
    $count = $count + 1
    f.util.print($count)
} while $count < 5
\end{verbatim}
The code block executes first, then the condition is checked. If the condition is \texttt{True}, the loop repeats. The same block-scoping rules as while loops apply: variables declared inside the code block are destroyed when the block exits. If the code block raises an error, the loop terminates immediately and the condition is not evaluated. \texttt{break} and \texttt{continue} work as expected inside do-while loops.

\subsection{Break and Continue}
\texttt{break} exits the innermost loop immediately. \texttt{continue} skips the remainder of the current iteration and proceeds to the next evaluation of the loop condition:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
break_stmt    = "break"
continue_stmt = "continue"

Example:
for $x E $values ::{
    if $x < 0 ::{
        continue
    }
    if $x > 100 ::{
        break
    }
    f.util.print($x)
}

Example with while:
$i [int]= 0
while $i < 10 ::{
    $i = $i + 1
    if $i % 2 == 0 ::{
        continue
    }
    f.util.print($i)
}
\end{verbatim}
\texttt{break} and \texttt{continue} apply only to the innermost enclosing loop.

\subsection{Nested While Loops}
While loops may be nested to arbitrary depth:
\begin{verbatim}
Example:
$rows [int]= 3
$cols [int]= 3
$i [int]= 0
while $i < $rows ::{
    $j [int]= 0
    while $j < $cols ::{
        f.util.print("Cell (" + f.str.str($i) + "," + f.str.str($j) + ")")
        $j = $j + 1
    }
    $i = $i + 1
}
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Safety: Maximum Iteration Limit}
To prevent infinite loops, the interpreter enforces a maximum iteration count per loop. The limit is controlled by \texttt{syst.flag.max\_loop\_iter} (default: 100000). If a loop exceeds this limit, a \texttt{Value Error} is raised:
\begin{verbatim}
Example:
syst.flag.max_loop_iter 500
$i [int]= 0
while $i < 1000 ::{
    $i = $i + 1
}
f.util.print($i)  # never reached — Value Error at 501 iterations
\end{verbatim}
The limit applies independently to each loop. Nested loops each have their own counter.

\subsection{Infinite Loops}
An infinite loop can be written with \texttt{True} as the condition. Use \texttt{break} to exit:
\begin{verbatim}
Example:
$count [int]= 0
while True ::{
    $count = $count + 1
    if $count == 10 ::{
        break
    }
}
f.util.print($count)   #c# Prints: 10
\end{verbatim}

\section{Goto Statements}
Goto statements provide unconditional jumps to labeled positions:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
gotomarker = "gotomarker", id
gotojump   = "gotojump", id

Example:
gotojump skip_section
f.util.print("This will not be printed")
gotomarker skip_section
f.util.print("Jumped here")
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Warning -- Deprecated Usage}
Goto statements are \textbf{strongly discouraged}. They make code difficult to read, debug, and maintain. Prefer structured control flow (\texttt{if}, \texttt{for}, \texttt{while}, \texttt{break}, \texttt{continue}) for all new code. Gotos are retained for backward compatibility only. Gotos may cross loop boundaries (e.g., jump from outside a loop into the loop body) but may not cross function boundaries.

\section{End of Program Statements}
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
halt = "halt"
noop = "skip"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{halt} -- Terminates program execution and exports all created files
\item \texttt{skip} -- No operation; program continues to next statement
\end{itemize}

\section{Output and Logging}
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
echo_statement = "echo", variable
writetolog     = "writetolog", string_expression

Example:
f.util.print("Hello, EDADSL!")
writetolog "Board design completed successfully"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{echo} -- Keyword that prints a variable reference to the console. Only accepts a variable, not an arbitrary expression. Use \texttt{f.util.print()} for expression output.
\item \texttt{writetolog} -- Keyword that appends a message to the log file
\end{itemize}




\chapter{Functions}
\section{Built-in Functions}

\textbf{Important:} All built-in function calls use a two-level namespace: \texttt{f.\textit{namespace}.\textit{function}}. This is defined in the formal grammar (Chapter~3):
\begin{verbatim}
function_call_built_in = "f", ".", namespace, ".", id, "(",
                        [{ data, "," }, data], ")"
namespace = "math"    | "trig"   | "hyp"  | "cplx" | "log"
          | "special" | "str"    | "list" | "tnsr"
          | "stat"    | "linalg" | "ee"   | "int"  | "util"
          | "seq"     | "sig"
\end{verbatim}
Correct usage: \texttt{f.math.abs(-3.2)}, \texttt{f.trig.sin(\$x)}, \texttt{f.tnsr.zeros([3,3])}\\
Incorrect: \texttt{f.math.abs(-3.2)}, \texttt{f.trig.sin(\$x)}, \texttt{abs(-3.2)}

\texttt{f.util.print()} takes a \textbf{single} argument. Use \texttt{f.str.str()} for string concatenation:
\begin{verbatim}
f.util.print("value = " + $x)              # OK
f.util.print(f.str.str("value = ", $x))    # OK
f.util.print("value = ", $x)               # WRONG - two arguments
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{Mathematical Functions}
\subsubsection{Absolute Value Function}
The \texttt{abs} function returns the absolute value of a numeric argument.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.abs", "(", arg, ")"
arg  =  bool | int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{bool}, \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item \texttt{bool} is coerced to \texttt{int}: \texttt{False} $\to$ \texttt{0}, \texttt{True} $\to$ \texttt{1}.
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from abs (e.g., \texttt{|-3| = |3|}).
\item Result: non-negative value of the same type (complex returns real, bool returns int).
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$ and $\mathbb{C}$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
|x| = \begin{cases} x & x \geq 0 \\ -x & x < 0 \end{cases}
\]

\paragraph{Complex Modulus}
When the Argument is a Complex Number $z = a + bi$, \texttt{abs} returns the Modulus:
\[
|a + bi| = \sqrt{a^2 + b^2}
\]
The Result is always a \texttt{real} Number, even if the Argument is Complex.

\paragraph{Tensor (Element-wise)}
For a Tensor $t$, \texttt{abs} is applied Element-wise:
\[
|t|[i] = |t[i]|
\]
The Output Tensor has the same Shape as the Input.

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Numerical Value of the Result:
\begin{itemize}
\item If the Result is Numerically Equal to an Integer, Return \texttt{int}
\item Otherwise, if the Result is Numerically Equal to a Real, Return \texttt{real}
\item Otherwise, Return \texttt{complex}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Non-negativity: $|x| \in \mathbb{R}^+_0$ for all $x$
\item Identity: $|x| = 0 \iff x = 0$
\item Multiplicativity: $|xy| = |x| \cdot |y|$
\item Triangle inequality: $|x + y| \leq |x| + |y|$
\item Symmetry: $|-x| = |x|$
\item Idempotence: $|(|x|)| = |x|$
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{bool} & \texttt{int} & \texttt{f.math.abs(True)} $\to$ \texttt{1} \\
\texttt{int} & \texttt{int} & \texttt{f.math.abs(-3)} $\to$ \texttt{3} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.math.abs(-3.2)} $\to$ \texttt{3.2} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.math.abs(3+4j)} $\to$ \texttt{5.0} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.math.abs([-1,2])} $\to$ \texttt{|[1,2]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.math.abs(|[-1,2])} $\to$ \texttt{|[1,2]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.math.abs(\{-3, 2\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{2, 3\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.abs(-3)              #c# Returns 3
f.math.abs(-3.2)            #c# Returns 3.2
f.math.abs(0)               #c# Returns 0
f.math.abs(-0.0)            #c# Returns 0.0
f.math.abs(42)              #c# Returns 42
f.math.abs(False)           #c# Returns 0
f.math.abs(True)            #c# Returns 1
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.abs(3 + 4j)          #c# Returns 5.0
f.math.abs(1 + 1j)          #c# Returns approximately 1.41421
f.math.abs(0 - 5j)          #c# Returns 5.0
f.math.abs(-3 - 4j)         #c# Returns 5.0
f.math.abs(0 + 0j)          #c# Returns 0.0
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.abs(|[-3, 0.2, 0, -7])   #c# Returns |[3, 0.2, 0, 7]
f.math.abs(|[[-1, 2], [3, -4]]) #c# Returns |[[1, 2], [3, 4]]
f.math.abs(|[-1.5, 2.5, -3.5])  #c# Returns |[1.5, 2.5, 3.5]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}
Numeric Lists are automatically converted to Tensors:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.abs([-3, 0.2, 0, -7])   #c# Returns |[3, 0.2, 0, 7]
f.math.abs([1, -2, 3, -4])     #c# Returns |[1, 2, 3, 4]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
Sets are processed element-wise. Duplicates may result from abs (e.g., \texttt{|-3| = |3|}):
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.abs({-3, 2, -1})        #c# Returns {1, 2, 3}
f.math.abs({-5, -5, 3})        #c# Returns {3, 5}
f.math.abs({0, -1, 1})         #c# Returns {0, 1}
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Numerical Considerations}
\begin{itemize}
\item For \texttt{real} inputs, \texttt{abs} is exact (no floating-point error introduced).
\item For \texttt{complex} inputs, the result may have floating-point error from the \texttt{sqrt} operation.
\item \texttt{-0.0} returns \texttt{0.0} (negative zero is normalized).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.abs("hello")         #c# Type Error
f.math.abs([1, "two", 3])   #c# Type Error (mixed list)
f.math.abs(c.math.rinf)     #c# Returns inf
f.math.abs(-c.math.rinf)    #c# Returns inf
f.math.abs(c.math.NaN)      #c# Returns NaN
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Parallel Equivalent Function}
The \texttt{parallel} function computes the parallel combination of a list or set of values---the standard formula for parallel resistances, conductances, or any set of admittances.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.parallel", "(", arg, ")"
arg  =  list | tensor | set
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{list}, \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{set} of \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, or \texttt{complex}.
\item Single element: returns that element unchanged (after \texttt{softint}(\texttt{softreal}())).
\item If any element equals zero, returns \texttt{0} immediately (no division by zero).
\item Infinite elements: ignored (removed before computation). If all elements are infinite, returns \texttt{inf}.
\item NaN values: propagate through result (returns \texttt{NaN}).
\item Mixed types: coerced to \texttt{complex}.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates are removed before computation.
\item Domain: all non-empty lists/tensors/sets of numeric values.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\text{parallel}([v_1, v_2, \ldots, v_n]) = \frac{1}{\frac{1}{v_1} + \frac{1}{v_2} + \cdots + \frac{1}{v_n}}
\]

\paragraph{Equivalent Forms}
\[
\text{parallel}([v_1, v_2]) = \frac{v_1 \cdot v_2}{v_1 + v_2}
\]
\[
\text{parallel}([v_1, v_2, \ldots, v_n]) = \left(\sum_{i=1}^{n} \frac{1}{v_i}\right)^{-1}
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Commutativity: parallel([a, b]) = parallel([b, a])
\item Associativity: parallel([a, parallel([b, c])]) = parallel([parallel([a, b]), c])
\item Identity: parallel([v, inf]) = v
\item Zero: parallel([v, 0]) = 0
\item Two equal: parallel([v, v]) = v/2
\item Three equal: parallel([v, v, v]) = v/3
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Numerical Value of the Result:
\begin{itemize}
\item If the Result is Numerically Equal to an Integer, Return \texttt{int}
\item Otherwise, if the Result is Numerically Equal to a Real, Return \texttt{real}
\item Otherwise, Return \texttt{complex}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.parallel([100, 100])          #c# Returns 50
f.math.parallel([100, 200])          #c# Returns approximately 66.666...
f.math.parallel([100, 200, 300])     #c# Returns approximately 54.5454...
f.math.parallel([1e3, 2.2e3])        #c# Returns 687.5
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Zero and Infinity}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.parallel([100, 0])            #c# Returns 0 (any zero)
f.math.parallel([100, 200, 0, 300])  #c# Returns 0 (any zero)
f.math.parallel([100, inf])          #c# Returns 100 (inf ignored)
f.math.parallel([100, 200, inf])     #c# Returns approximately 66.666... (inf ignored)
f.math.parallel([inf, inf, inf])     #c# Returns inf (all infinite)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.parallel([100 + 0j, 200 + 0j])   #c# Returns approximately 66.666... (real)
f.math.parallel([3 + 4j, 1 - 2j])       #c# Returns complex result
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates are removed before computation:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.parallel({100, 100})        #c# Returns 50 (duplicates removed)
f.math.parallel({100, 200, 300})   #c# Returns approximately 54.5454...
f.math.parallel({100, 0, 300})     #c# Returns 0 (any zero)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.parallel([])                #c# Value Error (empty list)
f.math.parallel([42])              #c# Returns 42 (single element, softint applied)
f.math.parallel([100, nan, 200])   #c# Returns NaN (NaN propagates)
f.math.parallel([100, "two"])      #c# Type Error (non-numeric)
f.math.parallel([100, 2], 3)       #c# Type Error (too many arguments)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Signum Function}
The \texttt{sign} function returns the Sign of the Argument: \texttt{-1} for negative, \texttt{0} for zero, \texttt{1} for positive.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.sign", "(", arg, ")"
arg  =  bool | int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{bool}, \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item \texttt{bool} is coerced to \texttt{int}: \texttt{False} $\to$ \texttt{0}, \texttt{True} $\to$ \texttt{1}.
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from sign.
\item Complex: returns unit complex $z / |z|$ (or \texttt{0} for $z = 0$).
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$ and $\mathbb{C}$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\text{sign}(x) = \begin{cases} 1 & x > 0 \\ 0 & x = 0 \\ -1 & x < 0 \end{cases}
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
\[
\text{sign}(z) = \begin{cases} \frac{z}{|z|} & z \neq 0 \\ 0 & z = 0 \end{cases}
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Odd function: $\text{sign}(-x) = -\text{sign}(x)$
\item Idempotence: $\text{sign}(\text{sign}(x)) = \text{sign}(x)$ for real $x$
\item Product: $\text{sign}(x) \cdot \text{sign}(y) = \text{sign}(x \cdot y)$
\item Magnitude: $|\text{sign}(x)| \leq 1$ for all $x$
\item Complex magnitude: $|\text{sign}(z)| = 1$ for $z \neq 0$
\item Equivalent to three-way compare: $\text{sign}(a) = a \texttt{ >< } 0$
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{bool} & \texttt{int} & \texttt{f.math.sign(True)} $\to$ \texttt{1} \\
\texttt{int} & \texttt{int} & \texttt{f.math.sign(-3)} $\to$ \texttt{-1} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{int} & \texttt{f.math.sign(-3.2)} $\to$ \texttt{-1} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.math.sign(3+4j)} $\to$ \texttt{0.6+0.8j} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.math.sign([-1,2])} $\to$ \texttt{|[-1,1]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.math.sign(|[-1,2])} $\to$ \texttt{|[-1,1]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.math.sign(\{-3, 2\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{-1, 1\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sign(-3)              #c# Returns -1
f.math.sign(-3.2)            #c# Returns -1
f.math.sign(0)               #c# Returns 0
f.math.sign(-0.0)            #c# Returns 0
f.math.sign(42)              #c# Returns 1
f.math.sign(False)           #c# Returns 0
f.math.sign(True)            #c# Returns 1
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sign(3 + 4j)          #c# Returns approximately 0.6 + 0.8j
f.math.sign(1 + 1j)          #c# Returns approximately 0.7071 + 0.7071j
f.math.sign(0 - 5j)          #c# Returns -1j
f.math.sign(-3 - 4j)         #c# Returns approximately -0.6 - 0.8j
f.math.sign(0 + 0j)          #c# Returns 0 (int)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sign(|[-3, 0.2, 0, -7])   #c# Returns |[-1, 1, 0, -1]
f.math.sign(|[[-1, 2], [3, -4]]) #c# Returns |[[-1, 1], [1, -1]]
f.math.sign(|[-1.5, 2.5, -3.5])  #c# Returns |[-1, 1, -1]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}
Numeric Lists are automatically converted to Tensors:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sign([-3, 0.2, 0, -7])   #c# Returns |[-1, 1, 0, -1]
f.math.sign([1, -2, 3, -4])     #c# Returns |[1, -1, 1, -1]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
Sets are processed element-wise. Duplicates may result from sign (e.g., \texttt{sign(-3) = sign(-5) = -1}):
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sign({-3, 2, -1})        #c# Returns {-1, 1}
f.math.sign({-5, -5, 3})        #c# Returns {-1, 1}
f.math.sign({0, -1, 1})         #c# Returns {-1, 0, 1}
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sign([])                #c# Value Error (empty list)
f.math.sign([42])              #c# Returns 1 (single element)
f.math.sign([100, nan, 200])   #c# Returns NaN (NaN propagates)
f.math.sign([100, "two"])      #c# Type Error (non-numeric)
f.math.sign([100, 2], 3)       #c# Type Error (too many arguments)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Heaviside Step Function}
The \texttt{hstep} function returns \texttt{0} for negative arguments, \texttt{1} for positive arguments, and a configurable value at zero.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.hstep", "(", arg, [",", at_zero], ")"
arg      =  bool | int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
at_zero  =  bool | int | real | complex | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{bool}, \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set} (\texttt{arg}); optional \texttt{bool}, \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list} (\texttt{at\_zero}, default \texttt{0.5}).
\item \texttt{bool} is coerced to \texttt{int}: \texttt{False} $\to$ \texttt{0}, \texttt{True} $\to$ \texttt{1}.
\item Result: \texttt{real} (or \texttt{complex} if \texttt{arg} is complex). Applies \texttt{softint} and \texttt{softreal}: returns \texttt{int} if numerically equal to an integer, otherwise \texttt{real}.
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from hstep.
\item Complex: thresholds on real part: $\text{Re}(z) > 0 \to 1$, $\text{Re}(z) < 0 \to 0$, $\text{Re}(z) = 0 \to \texttt{at\_zero}$.
\item \texttt{at\_zero} controls the return value when the argument is zero.
\item If \texttt{at\_zero} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{at\_zero} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{at\_zero} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$ and $\mathbb{C}$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\text{hstep}(x) = \begin{cases} 0 & x < 0 \\ \texttt{at\_zero} & x = 0 \\ 1 & x > 0 \end{cases}
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
For complex arguments, thresholds on the real part:
\[
\text{hstep}(z) = \begin{cases} 1 & \text{Re}(z) > 0 \\ \texttt{at\_zero} & \text{Re}(z) = 0 \\ 0 & \text{Re}(z) < 0 \end{cases}
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Right-continuous: $\text{hstep}(0, 0) = 0$
\item Left-continuous: $\text{hstep}(0, 1) = 1$
\item Standard math: $\text{hstep}(0) = 0.5$ (default)
\item Complement: $\text{hstep}(x) + \text{hstep}(-x) = 1$ for $x \neq 0$
\item Product: $\text{hstep}(x) \cdot \text{hstep}(y) = \text{hstep}(\min(x, y))$
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Argument:
\begin{itemize}
\item If $x \neq 0$: returns \texttt{int} (\texttt{0} or \texttt{1})
\item If $x = 0$: returns \texttt{f.util.type(at\_zero)} (default \texttt{0.5} is \texttt{real})
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{bool} (\texttt{True}) & \texttt{int} & \texttt{f.math.hstep(True)} $\to$ \texttt{1} \\
\texttt{bool} (\texttt{False}) & \texttt{f.util.type(at\_zero)} & \texttt{f.math.hstep(False)} $\to$ \texttt{0.5} \\
\texttt{int} ($x \neq 0$) & \texttt{int} & \texttt{f.math.hstep(-3)} $\to$ \texttt{0} \\
\texttt{int} ($x = 0$) & \texttt{f.util.type(at\_zero)} & \texttt{f.math.hstep(0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.5} \\
\texttt{real} ($x \neq 0$) & \texttt{int} & \texttt{f.math.hstep(0.0)} $\to$ \texttt{1} \\
\texttt{real} ($x = 0$) & \texttt{f.util.type(at\_zero)} & \texttt{f.math.hstep(0.0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.5} \\
\texttt{complex} ($z \neq 0$) & \texttt{int} & \texttt{f.math.hstep(3+4j)} $\to$ \texttt{1} \\
\texttt{complex} ($z = 0$) & \texttt{f.util.type(at\_zero)} & \texttt{f.math.hstep(0+0j)} $\to$ \texttt{0.5} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.math.hstep([-1,1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0,1]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.math.hstep(|[-1,1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0,1]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.math.hstep(\{-1, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0, 1\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.hstep(-3)              #c# Returns 0
f.math.hstep(-3.2)            #c# Returns 0
f.math.hstep(0)               #c# Returns 0.5
f.math.hstep(0.0)             #c# Returns 0.5
f.math.hstep(5)               #c# Returns 1
f.math.hstep(False)           #c# Returns 0
f.math.hstep(True)            #c# Returns 1
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{at\_zero Parameter}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.hstep(0, 0)                     #c# Returns 0 (right-continuous)
f.math.hstep(0, 1)                     #c# Returns 1 (left-continuous)
f.math.hstep(0, 0.5)                   #c# Returns 0.5 (standard)
f.math.hstep([-2, 0, 3])               #c# Returns |[0, 0.5, 1]
f.math.hstep([-2, 0, 3], 0)            #c# Returns |[0, 0, 1]
f.math.hstep([-1, 0, 1], [0, 0.5, 1])  #c# Returns |[0, 0.5, 1] (tensor at_zero)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.hstep(3 + 4j)          #c# Returns 1 (Re > 0)
f.math.hstep(-3 + 4j)         #c# Returns 0 (Re < 0)
f.math.hstep(0 + 4j)          #c# Returns 0.5 (Re = 0, at_zero is real)
f.math.hstep(0 + 4j, 0)       #c# Returns 0 (Re = 0, at_zero=0)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.hstep(|[-3, 0.2, 0, -7])   #c# Returns |[0, 1, 0.5, 0]
f.math.hstep(|[[-1, 2], [3, -4]]) #c# Returns |[[0, 1], [1, 0]]
f.math.hstep(|[-1.5, 2.5, -3.5])  #c# Returns |[0, 1, 0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}
Numeric Lists are automatically converted to Tensors:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.hstep([-3, 0.2, 0, -7])   #c# Returns |[0, 1, 0.5, 0]
f.math.hstep([1, -2, 3, -4])     #c# Returns |[1, 0, 1, 0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
Sets are processed element-wise. Duplicates may result from hstep (e.g., \texttt{hstep(-3) = hstep(-5) = 0}):
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.hstep({-3, 2, -1})        #c# Returns {0, 1}
f.math.hstep({-5, -5, 3})        #c# Returns {0, 1}
f.math.hstep({0, -1, 1})         #c# Returns {0, 0.5, 1}
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.hstep([])                #c# Value Error (empty list)
f.math.hstep([42])              #c# Returns 1 (single element)
f.math.hstep([100, nan, 200])   #c# Returns NaN (NaN propagates)
f.math.hstep([100, "two"])      #c# Type Error (non-numeric)
f.math.hstep([100, 2], 3, 4)    #c# Type Error (too many arguments)
f.math.hstep([1, 2], [1])       #c# Runtime Error (at_zero shape mismatch)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Sigmoid Function}
The \texttt{sigmoid} function computes a smooth approximation of the Heaviside step function. It maps any real number to a bounded range, with selectable curve types.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.sigmoid", "(", arg,
  [",", "min", "=", min_val],
  [",", "max", "=", max_val],
  [",", "curve", "=", curve], ")"
arg      =  int | real | tensor | list | set
min_val  =  int | real | tensor | list
max_val  =  int | real | tensor | list
curve    =  string
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item First argument (\texttt{arg}): input value (\texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}).
\item \texttt{min} (default \texttt{0.0}): lower bound of output range. Can be \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item \texttt{max} (default \texttt{1.0}): upper bound of output range. Can be \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item \texttt{curve} (default \texttt{"logistic"}): sigmoid curve type (see table below).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Tensors are processed element-wise.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from sigmoid.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Standard Sigmoid}
\[
\sigma(x) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-x}}
\]

\paragraph{Scaled Sigmoid}
\[
\sigma_{\text{scaled}}(x) = \texttt{min} + (\texttt{max} - \texttt{min}) \cdot \sigma(x)
\]

\paragraph{Curve Selection}
All curves are S-shaped, monotonically increasing, and bounded.
\begin{center}
\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.5}
\small
\begin{tabular}{llllccc}
\textbf{Curve} & \textbf{Alias} & \textbf{Formula} & \textbf{Range} & \textbf{Cost} & \textbf{Smooth} & $\boldsymbol{\sigma'(0)}$ \\ \hline
\texttt{"logistic"} & \texttt{"lg"} & $\frac{1}{1 + \exp\left(-x\right)}$ & $(0, 1)$ & Medium & C$^\infty$ & 0.25 \\
\texttt{"tanh"} & \texttt{"th"} & $\tanh\left(x\right)$ & $(-1, 1)$ & Medium & C$^\infty$ & 1.0 \\
\texttt{"softsign"} & \texttt{"ss"} & $\frac{x}{1 + \left|x\right|}$ & $(-1, 1)$ & Cheap & C$^0$ & 1.0 \\
\texttt{"arctan"} & \texttt{"at"} & $\frac{2}{\pi} \arctan\left(x\right)$ & $(-1, 1)$ & High med. & C$^\infty$ & $\frac{2}{\pi}$ \\
\texttt{"algebraic"} & \texttt{"al"} & $\frac{x}{\sqrt{1 + x^2}}$ & $(-1, 1)$ & Low med. & C$^\infty$ & 1.0 \\
\texttt{"hard"} & \texttt{"hr"} & $\begin{cases} -1 & x < -1 \\ 1 & x > 1 \\ x & \text{otherwise} \end{cases}$ & $(-1, 1)$ & Very cheap & C$^0$ & 1.0 \\
\texttt{"erf"} & \texttt{"er"} & $\text{erf}\left(x\right)$ & $(-1, 1)$ & High med. & C$^\infty$ & $\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}$ \\
\texttt{"smoothstep"} & \texttt{"s3"} & $3x^2 - 2x^3$ & $(0, 1)$ & Cheap & C$^2$ & 0.0 \\
\texttt{"smootherstep"} & \texttt{"s5"} & $6x^5 - 15x^4 + 10x^3$ & $(0, 1)$ & Cheap & C$^2$ & 0.0 \\
\texttt{"smooth+"} & \texttt{"s+"} & $\frac{x}{1 + \ln\left(1 + \exp\left(\sqrt{1+x^2}\right)\right)}$ & $(-1, 1)$ & Very exp. & C$^\infty$ & $\approx 0.432$ \\
\texttt{"sqrt"} & \texttt{"rt"} & $\frac{x}{\sqrt{x^2+1}+1}$ & $(-1, 1)$ & Low med. & C$^\infty$ & 0.5 \\
\texttt{"quartic"} & \texttt{"qr"} & $\frac{x}{\sqrt{1+x^4}}$ & $(-1, 1)$ & Low med. & C$^\infty$ & 1.0 \\
\texttt{"exponential"} & \texttt{"ex"} & $\text{sign}\left(x\right) \cdot \left(1 - \exp\left(-\left|x\right|\right)\right)$ & $(-1, 1)$ & Medium & C$^0$ & 1.0 \\
\texttt{"gudermannian"} & \texttt{"gd"} & $\frac{2}{\pi} \cdot \text{gd}\left(x\right)$ & $(-1, 1)$ & Expensive & C$^\infty$ & $\frac{2}{\pi}$ \\
\texttt{"arcalg"} & \texttt{"aa"} & $\frac{\pi}{2} \cdot \frac{x}{1 + x \cdot \arctan\left(x\right)}$ & $(-1, 1)$ & Medium & C$^\infty$ & $\frac{\pi}{2}$ \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Numerical Stability}
For large negative $x$, direct computation may underflow. Use the equivalent form:
\[
\sigma(x) = \begin{cases} \frac{1}{1 + e^{-x}} & x \geq 0 \\ \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} & x < 0 \end{cases}
\]

\paragraph{Optimization}
For $|x| > T$, where $T$ is \texttt{syst.flag.sigmoid\_optim\_threshold} (default: 150), the result is clamped to \texttt{min} or \texttt{max} directly, bypassing curve evaluation:
\[
\sigma(x) = \begin{cases} \texttt{min} & x < -T \\ \texttt{max} & x > T \\ \text{curve}(x) & \text{otherwise} \end{cases}
\]
Setting $T = 0$ disables the optimization. A \texttt{Warning} is raised when $T < 10$ because small thresholds create visible discontinuities at the clamping boundary---the curve value at $x = T$ may differ significantly from \texttt{min} or \texttt{max}.

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Default range: $(0, 1)$
\item Custom range: $(\texttt{min}, \texttt{max})$
\item Centered at: $\sigma(0) = \frac{\texttt{min} + \texttt{max}}{2}$
\item Symmetry: $\sigma(-x) = \texttt{min} + \texttt{max} - \sigma(x)$
\item Monotonic: $\sigma(x_1) < \sigma(x_2)$ for $x_1 < x_2$
\item Bounded: $\texttt{min} \leq \sigma(x) \leq \texttt{max}$
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item Scalar input: returns \texttt{real} (or \texttt{int} if result is numerically equal to an integer)
\item Tensor input: returns \texttt{tensor} of \texttt{real}
\item List input: returns \texttt{tensor} of \texttt{real} (after auto-conversion)
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set} of \texttt{real}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.math.sigmoid(0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.5} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.math.sigmoid(0.0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.5} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.math.sigmoid([-2,2])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.1192, 0.8808]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.math.sigmoid(|[-2,2])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.1192, 0.8808]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.math.sigmoid(\{-2, 2\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0.1192, 0.8808\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sigmoid(0)                     #c# Returns midpoint (min + max) / 2
f.math.sigmoid(100)                   #c# Returns max (clamped at threshold)
f.math.sigmoid(-100)                  #c# Returns min (clamped at threshold)
f.math.sigmoid(0, min=1, max=0)       #c# Value Error (min > max)
f.math.sigmoid(0, curve="unknown")    #c# Value Error (unknown curve)
f.math.sigmoid("hello")               #c# Type Error (non-numeric)
f.math.sigmoid([1, "two"])            #c# Type Error (mixed list)
f.math.sigmoid([], min=0, max=1)      #c# Value Error (empty list)
syst.flag.sigmoid_optim_threshold -1  #c# Value Error (negative threshold)
syst.flag.sigmoid_optim_threshold 1   #c# Warning (threshold < 10)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sigmoid(0)              #c# Returns 0.5
f.math.sigmoid(2)              #c# Returns approximately 0.8808
f.math.sigmoid(-2)             #c# Returns approximately 0.1192
f.math.sigmoid(100)            #c# Returns 1.0 (clamped)
f.math.sigmoid(-100)           #c# Returns 0.0 (clamped)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Scaled Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sigmoid(0, min=-1, max=1)    #c# Returns 0.0
f.math.sigmoid(2, min=-1, max=1)    #c# Returns approximately 0.7616
f.math.sigmoid(-2, min=-1, max=1)   #c# Returns approximately -0.7616
f.math.sigmoid(0, min=0, max=100)   #c# Returns 50.0
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Curve Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sigmoid(0, curve="tanh")          #c# Returns 0.0
f.math.sigmoid(2, curve="tanh")          #c# Returns approximately 0.9640
f.math.sigmoid(0, curve="erf")           #c# Returns 0.0
f.math.sigmoid(2, curve="erf")           #c# Returns approximately 0.9953
f.math.sigmoid(0, curve="gd")            #c# Returns 0.0
f.math.sigmoid(2, curve="gd")            #c# Returns approximately 1.3018
f.math.sigmoid(0, curve="smooth+")       #c# Returns 0.0
f.math.sigmoid(2, curve="smooth+")       #c# Returns approximately 0.8808
f.math.sigmoid(-2, curve="smooth+")      #c# Returns approximately -0.8808
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Alias Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sigmoid(2, curve="lg")            #c# Same as logistic
f.math.sigmoid(2, curve="th")            #c# Same as tanh
f.math.sigmoid(2, curve="er")            #c# Same as erf
f.math.sigmoid(2, curve="gd")            #c# Same as gudermannian
f.math.sigmoid(2, curve="s+")            #c# Same as smooth+
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sigmoid(|[-2, 0, 2])                 #c# Returns |[0.1192, 0.5, 0.8808]
f.math.sigmoid(|[-2, 0, 2], min=-1, max=1)  #c# Returns |[-0.7616, 0.0, 0.7616]
f.math.sigmoid(|[-2, 0, 2], curve="erf")    #c# Returns |[0.0455, 0.0, 0.9953]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sigmoid([-2, 0, 2])              #c# Returns |[0.1192, 0.5, 0.8808]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.sigmoid({-2, 0, 2})              #c# Returns {0.1192, 0.5, 0.8808}
f.math.sigmoid({-2, 2})                 #c# Returns {0.1192, 0.8808}
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Threshold Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
# Default threshold (150)
f.math.sigmoid(151)                   #c# Returns 1.0 (clamped)
f.math.sigmoid(149)                   #c# Returns approximately 1.0 (curve evaluated)

# Custom threshold
syst.flag.sigmoid_optim_threshold 10
f.math.sigmoid(11)                    #c# Returns 1.0 (clamped at 10)
f.math.sigmoid(9)                     #c# Returns approximately 1.0 (curve evaluated)

# Disable optimization
syst.flag.sigmoid_optim_threshold 0
f.math.sigmoid(200)                   #c# Returns approximately 1.0 (always curve evaluated)

# Low threshold -- triggers warning
syst.flag.sigmoid_optim_threshold 1   #c# Warning: threshold < 10 may cause discontinuities
f.math.sigmoid(2)                     #c# Returns 1.0 (clamped, but sigma(1) approximately 0.731 -- 27% jump)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Sine Function}
Computes the sine of an angle. Angles are interpreted as radians by default.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.sin", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from sin.
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$ and all $\mathbb{C}$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\sin(x) = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \frac{(-1)^n x^{2n+1}}{(2n+1)!}
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
For complex arguments $z = a + bi$:
\[
\sin(a + bi) = \sin(a)\cosh(b) + i\cos(a)\sinh(b)
\]
Special cases:
\[
\sin(iy) = i\sinh(y)
\]
\[
\sin(a) \in \mathbb{R} \text{ for } a \in \mathbb{R}
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\sin_{\text{deg}}(x) = \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Periodicity: $\sin(x + 2\pi) = \sin(x)$
\item Odd function: $\sin(-x) = -\sin(x)$
\item Pythagorean: $\sin^2(x) + \cos^2(x) = 1$
\item Range: $[-1, 1]$ for real $x$
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.sin(1)} $\to$ \texttt{0.8415} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.sin(1.0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.8415} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.sin(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{3.1658-1.9596j} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.sin([0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 0.8415]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.sin(|[0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 0.8415]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.sin(\{0, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0.0, 0.8415\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sin(0)                #c# Returns 0.0
f.trig.sin(1.570796327)      #c# Returns approximately 1.0
f.trig.sin(3.141592654)      #c# Returns approximately 0.0
f.trig.sin(-1.570796327)     #c# Returns approximately -1.0
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Degree Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sin(0, unit="deg")     #c# Returns 0.0
f.trig.sin(90, unit="deg")    #c# Returns 1.0
f.trig.sin(180, unit="deg")   #c# Returns approximately 0.0
f.trig.sin(-90, unit="deg")   #c# Returns -1.0
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sin(1 + 2j)           #c# Returns approximately 3.1658 - 1.9596j
f.trig.sin(0 + 1j)           #c# Returns approximately 1.1752j
f.trig.sin(1 + 0j)           #c# Returns approximately 0.8415 + 0j
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sin(|[0, 1.5708, 3.1416])       #c# Returns |[0.0, 1.0, 0.0]
f.trig.sin(|[0, 90, 180], unit="deg")  #c# Returns |[0.0, 1.0, 0.0]
f.trig.sin(|[[0, 1], [2, 3]])          #c# Returns |[[0.0, 0.8415], [0.9093, 0.1411]]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}
Numeric Lists are automatically converted to Tensors:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sin([0, 1.5708, 3.1416])         #c# Returns |[0.0, 1.0, 0.0]
f.trig.sin([0, 30, 60, 90], unit="deg") #c# Returns |[0.0, 0.5, 0.8660, 1.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Mixed Unit Examples}
When \texttt{unit} is a list, each element uses its corresponding unit:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sin([90, 1.5708], unit=["deg", "rad"])  #c# Returns |[1.0, 1.0]
f.trig.sin([180, 3.1416], unit=["deg", "rad"]) #c# Returns |[0.0, 0.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
Sets are processed element-wise. Duplicates may result from sin:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sin({0, 1})            #c# Returns {0.0, 0.8415}
f.trig.sin({0, 3.141592654})  #c# Returns {0.0} (duplicates removed)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sin([])                    #c# Value Error (empty list)
f.trig.sin([1, "two"])            #c# Type Error (non-numeric)
f.trig.sin([1, 2], 3, 4)          #c# Type Error (too many arguments)
f.trig.sin(1, unit="invalid")     #c# Value Error (invalid unit)
f.trig.sin([1, 2], unit=["deg"])  #c# Runtime Error (unit shape mismatch)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Cosine Function}
Computes the cosine of an angle. Angles are interpreted as radians by default.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.cos", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from cos.
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$ and all $\mathbb{C}$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\cos(x) = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \frac{(-1)^n x^{2n}}{(2n)!}
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
For complex arguments $z = a + bi$:
\[
\cos(a + bi) = \cos(a)\cosh(b) - i\sin(a)\sinh(b)
\]
Special cases:
\[
\cos(iy) = \cosh(y)
\]
\[
\cos(a) \in \mathbb{R} \text{ for } a \in \mathbb{R}
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\cos_{\text{deg}}(x) = \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Periodicity: $\cos(x + 2\pi) = \cos(x)$
\item Even function: $\cos(-x) = \cos(x)$
\item Pythagorean: $\sin^2(x) + \cos^2(x) = 1$
\item Range: $[-1, 1]$ for real $x$
\item Phase shift: $\cos(x) = \sin(x + \frac{\pi}{2})$
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.cos(0)} $\to$ \texttt{1.0} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.cos(0.0)} $\to$ \texttt{1.0} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.cos(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{2.0327+3.0519j} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.cos([0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[1.0, 0.5403]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.cos(|[0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[1.0, 0.5403]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.cos(\{0, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0.5403, 1.0\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cos(0)                #c# Returns 1.0
f.trig.cos(1.570796327)      #c# Returns approximately 0.0
f.trig.cos(3.141592654)      #c# Returns approximately -1.0
f.trig.cos(-1.570796327)     #c# Returns approximately 0.0
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Degree Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cos(0, unit="deg")     #c# Returns 1.0
f.trig.cos(90, unit="deg")    #c# Returns approximately 0.0
f.trig.cos(180, unit="deg")   #c# Returns approximately -1.0
f.trig.cos(-90, unit="deg")   #c# Returns approximately 0.0
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cos(1 + 2j)           #c# Returns approximately 2.0327 - 3.0519j
f.trig.cos(0 + 1j)           #c# Returns approximately 1.5431
f.trig.cos(1 + 0j)           #c# Returns approximately 0.5403 + 0j
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cos(|[0, 1.5708, 3.1416])       #c# Returns |[1.0, 0.0, -1.0]
f.trig.cos(|[0, 90, 180], unit="deg")  #c# Returns |[1.0, 0.0, -1.0]
f.trig.cos(|[[0, 1], [2, 3]])          #c# Returns |[[1.0, 0.5403], [-0.4161, -0.9900]]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}
Numeric Lists are automatically converted to Tensors:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cos([0, 1.5708, 3.1416])       #c# Returns |[1.0, 0.0, -1.0]
f.trig.cos([0, 60, 90], unit="deg")   #c# Returns |[1.0, 0.5, 0.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Mixed Unit Examples}
When \texttt{unit} is a list, each element uses its corresponding unit:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cos([0, 0], unit=["deg", "rad"])        #c# Returns |[1.0, 1.0]
f.trig.cos([180, 3.1416], unit=["deg", "rad"]) #c# Returns |[-1.0, -1.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
Sets are processed element-wise. Duplicates may result from cos:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cos({0, 1})            #c# Returns {0.5403, 1.0}
f.trig.cos({0, 6.283185307})  #c# Returns {1.0} (duplicates removed)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cos([])                    #c# Value Error (empty list)
f.trig.cos([1, "two"])            #c# Type Error (non-numeric)
f.trig.cos([1, 2], 3, 4)          #c# Type Error (too many arguments)
f.trig.cos(1, unit="invalid")     #c# Value Error (invalid unit)
f.trig.cos([1, 2], unit=["deg"])  #c# Runtime Error (unit shape mismatch)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Tangent Function}
Computes the tangent of an angle. Angles are interpreted as radians by default.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.tan", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from tan.
\item Domain: $\{x \in \mathbb{C} : \cos x \neq 0\}$. A \texttt{Division by Zero Error} is raised when $\cos x = 0$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\tan(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)}
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
For complex arguments $z = a + bi$:
\[
\tan(a + bi) = \frac{\sin(2a) + i\sinh(2b)}{\cos(2a) + \cosh(2b)}
\]
Special cases:
\[
\tan(iy) = i\tanh(y)
\]
\[
\tan(a) \in \mathbb{R} \text{ for } a \in \mathbb{R}, \cos(a) \neq 0
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\tan_{\text{deg}}(x) = \tan\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Periodicity: $\tan(x + \pi) = \tan(x)$
\item Odd function: $\tan(-x) = -\tan(x)$
\item Pythagorean: $1 + \tan^2(x) = \sec^2(x)$
\item Domain: $\{x \in \mathbb{C} : \cos x \neq 0\}$
\item Identity: $\tan(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)}$
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.tan(0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.0} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.tan(0.0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.0} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.tan(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{-0.2434+1.1668j} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.tan([0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 1.5574]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.tan(|[0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 1.5574]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.tan(\{0, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0.0, 1.5574\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.tan(0)                #c# Returns 0.0
f.trig.tan(0.785398163)      #c# Returns approximately 1.0
f.trig.tan(1.570796327)      #c# Returns inf (cos = 0)
f.trig.tan(-0.785398163)     #c# Returns approximately -1.0
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Degree Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.tan(0, unit="deg")     #c# Returns 0.0
f.trig.tan(45, unit="deg")    #c# Returns 1.0
f.trig.tan(90, unit="deg")    #c# Returns inf (cos = 0)
f.trig.tan(-45, unit="deg")   #c# Returns -1.0
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.tan(1 + 2j)           #c# Returns approximately -0.2434 + 1.1668j
f.trig.tan(0 + 1j)           #c# Returns approximately 0.7616j
f.trig.tan(1 + 0j)           #c# Returns approximately 1.5574 + 0j
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.tan(|[0, 0.7854, 1.5708])       #c# Returns |[0.0, 1.0, inf]
f.trig.tan(|[0, 45, 90], unit="deg")   #c# Returns |[0.0, 1.0, inf]
f.trig.tan(|[[0, 1], [2, 3]])          #c# Returns |[[0.0, 1.5574], [-2.1850, -0.1425]]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}
Numeric Lists are automatically converted to Tensors:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.tan([0, 0.7854, 1.5708])       #c# Returns |[0.0, 1.0, inf]
f.trig.tan([0, 45, 90], unit="deg")   #c# Returns |[0.0, 1.0, inf]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Mixed Unit Examples}
When \texttt{unit} is a list, each element uses its corresponding unit:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.tan([0, 0], unit=["deg", "rad"])       #c# Returns |[0.0, 0.0]
f.trig.tan([45, 0.7854], unit=["deg", "rad"]) #c# Returns |[1.0, 1.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
Sets are processed element-wise. Duplicates may result from tan:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.tan({0, 1})            #c# Returns {0.0, 1.5574}
f.trig.tan({0, 3.141592654})  #c# Returns {0.0} (duplicates removed)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.tan([])                    #c# Value Error (empty list)
f.trig.tan([1, "two"])            #c# Type Error (non-numeric)
f.trig.tan([1, 2], 3, 4)          #c# Type Error (too many arguments)
f.trig.tan(1, unit="invalid")     #c# Value Error (invalid unit)
f.trig.tan([1, 2], unit=["deg"])  #c# Runtime Error (unit shape mismatch)
f.trig.tan(1.570796327)           #c# Division by Zero Error (cos = 0)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Cotangent Function}
Computes the cotangent of an angle. Angles are interpreted as radians by default.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.cot", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from cot.
\item Domain: $\{x \in \mathbb{C} : \sin x \neq 0\}$. A \texttt{Division by Zero Error} is raised when $\sin x = 0$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\cot(x) = \frac{\cos(x)}{\sin(x)} = \frac{1}{\tan(x)}
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
For complex arguments $z = a + bi$:
\[
\cot(a + bi) = \frac{\sin(2a) - i\sinh(2b)}{\cosh(2b) - \cos(2a)}
\]
Special cases:
\[
\cot(iy) = -i\coth(y)
\]
\[
\cot(a) \in \mathbb{R} \text{ for } a \in \mathbb{R}, \sin(a) \neq 0
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\cot_{\text{deg}}(x) = \cot\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Periodicity: $\cot(x + \pi) = \cot(x)$
\item Odd function: $\cot(-x) = -\cot(x)$
\item Pythagorean: $1 + \cot^2(x) = \csc^2(x)$
\item Reciprocal: $\cot(x) = \frac{1}{\tan(x)}$
\item Domain: $\{x \in \mathbb{C} : \sin x \neq 0\}$
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.cot(1)} $\to$ \texttt{0.6421} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.cot(0.7854)} $\to$ \texttt{1.0} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.cot(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{-0.0327-0.9903j} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.cot([0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[inf, 0.6421]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.cot(|[0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[inf, 0.6421]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.cot(\{0, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{inf, 0.6421\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cot(0.785398163)      #c# Returns approximately 1.0 (pi/4)
f.trig.cot(1.0)              #c# Returns approximately 0.6421
f.trig.cot(0.0)              #c# Returns inf (sin = 0)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Degree Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cot(45, unit="deg")    #c# Returns 1.0
f.trig.cot(90, unit="deg")    #c# Returns 0.0
f.trig.cot(0, unit="deg")     #c# Returns inf (sin = 0)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cot(1 + 2j)           #c# Returns approximately -0.0327 - 0.9903j
f.trig.cot(0 + 1j)           #c# Returns approximately -1.3130j
f.trig.cot(1 + 0j)           #c# Returns approximately 0.6421 + 0j
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cot(|[0.7854, 1.0])           #c# Returns |[1.0, 0.6421]
f.trig.cot(|[45, 90], unit="deg")    #c# Returns |[1.0, 0.0]
f.trig.cot(|[[0.7854, 1.0]])         #c# Returns |[[1.0, 0.6421]]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}
Numeric Lists are automatically converted to Tensors:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cot([0.7854, 1.0])          #c# Returns |[1.0, 0.6421]
f.trig.cot([45, 90], unit="deg")   #c# Returns |[1.0, 0.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Mixed Unit Examples}
When \texttt{unit} is a list, each element uses its corresponding unit:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cot([45, 0.7854], unit=["deg", "rad"]) #c# Returns |[1.0, 1.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
Sets are processed element-wise. Duplicates may result from cot:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cot({0.7854, 1.0})       #c# Returns {1.0, 0.6421}
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.cot([])                    #c# Value Error (empty list)
f.trig.cot([1, "two"])            #c# Type Error (non-numeric)
f.trig.cot([1, 2], 3, 4)          #c# Type Error (too many arguments)
f.trig.cot(1, unit="invalid")     #c# Value Error (invalid unit)
f.trig.cot([1, 2], unit=["deg"])  #c# Runtime Error (unit shape mismatch)
f.trig.cot(3.141592654)           #c# Division by Zero Error (sin = 0)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Secant Function}
Computes the secant of an angle. Angles are interpreted as radians by default.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.sec", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from sec.
\item Domain: $\{x \in \mathbb{C} : \cos x \neq 0\}$. A \texttt{Division by Zero Error} is raised when $\cos x = 0$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\sec(x) = \frac{1}{\cos(x)}
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
For complex arguments $z = a + bi$:
\[
\sec(a + bi) = \frac{2\cos(a)}{\cos(2a) + \cosh(2b)}
\]
Special cases:
\[
\sec(iy) = \frac{1}{\cosh(y)}
\]
\[
\sec(a) \in \mathbb{R} \text{ for } a \in \mathbb{R}, \cos(a) \neq 0
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\sec_{\text{deg}}(x) = \sec\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Reciprocal: $\sec(x) = \frac{1}{\cos(x)}$
\item Pythagorean: $\sec^2(x) = 1 + \tan^2(x)$
\item Periodicity: $\sec(x + 2\pi) = \sec(x)$
\item Even function: $\sec(-x) = \sec(x)$
\item Domain: $\{x \in \mathbb{C} : \cos x \neq 0\}$
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.sec(0)} $\to$ \texttt{1.0} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.sec(0.0)} $\to$ \texttt{1.0} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.sec(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{0.1414+0.4946j} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.sec([0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[1.0, 1.8508]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.sec(|[0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[1.0, 1.8508]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.sec(\{0, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{1.0, 1.8508\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sec(0.0)              #c# Returns 1.0
f.trig.sec(1.0)              #c# Returns approximately 1.8508
f.trig.sec(1.570796327)      #c# Returns inf (cos = 0)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Degree Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sec(0, unit="deg")     #c# Returns 1.0
f.trig.sec(60, unit="deg")    #c# Returns 2.0
f.trig.sec(90, unit="deg")    #c# Returns inf (cos = 0)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sec(1 + 2j)           #c# Returns approximately 0.1414 + 0.4946j
f.trig.sec(0 + 1j)           #c# Returns approximately 0.6481
f.trig.sec(1 + 0j)           #c# Returns approximately 1.8508 + 0j
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sec(|[0, 1.0, 1.5708])        #c# Returns |[1.0, 1.8508, inf]
f.trig.sec(|[0, 60, 90], unit="deg") #c# Returns |[1.0, 2.0, inf]
f.trig.sec(|[[0, 1], [2, 3]])        #c# Returns |[[1.0, 1.8508], [-2.4030, -1.0101]]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}
Numeric Lists are automatically converted to Tensors:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sec([0, 1.0, 1.5708])        #c# Returns |[1.0, 1.8508, inf]
f.trig.sec([0, 60, 90], unit="deg") #c# Returns |[1.0, 2.0, inf]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Mixed Unit Examples}
When \texttt{unit} is a list, each element uses its corresponding unit:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sec([0, 0], unit=["deg", "rad"])    #c# Returns |[1.0, 1.0]
f.trig.sec([60, 0.0], unit=["deg", "rad"]) #c# Returns |[2.0, 1.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
Sets are processed element-wise. Duplicates may result from sec:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sec({0, 1.0})       #c# Returns {1.0, 1.8508}
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.sec([])                    #c# Value Error (empty list)
f.trig.sec([1, "two"])            #c# Type Error (non-numeric)
f.trig.sec([1, 2], 3, 4)          #c# Type Error (too many arguments)
f.trig.sec(1, unit="invalid")     #c# Value Error (invalid unit)
f.trig.sec([1, 2], unit=["deg"])  #c# Runtime Error (unit shape mismatch)
f.trig.sec(1.570796327)           #c# Division by Zero Error (cos = 0)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Cosecant Function}
Computes the cosecant of an angle. Angles are interpreted as radians by default.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.csc", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from csc.
\item Domain: $\{x \in \mathbb{C} : \sin x \neq 0\}$. A \texttt{Division by Zero Error} is raised when $\sin x = 0$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\csc(x) = \frac{1}{\sin(x)}
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
For complex arguments $z = a + bi$:
\[
\csc(a + bi) = \frac{2\sin(a)}{\cos(2a) - \cosh(2b)}
\]
Special cases:
\[
\csc(iy) = -i\operatorname{csch}(y)
\]
\[
\csc(a) \in \mathbb{R} \text{ for } a \in \mathbb{R}, \sin(a) \neq 0
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\csc_{\text{deg}}(x) = \csc\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Reciprocal: $\csc(x) = \frac{1}{\sin(x)}$
\item Pythagorean: $\csc^2(x) = 1 + \cot^2(x)$
\item Periodicity: $\csc(x + 2\pi) = \csc(x)$
\item Odd function: $\csc(-x) = -\csc(x)$
\item Domain: $\{x \in \mathbb{C} : \sin x \neq 0\}$
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.csc(1)} $\to$ \texttt{1.1884} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.csc(1.0)} $\to$ \texttt{1.1884} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.csc(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{0.0998-0.2033j} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.csc([1, 2])} $\to$ \texttt{|[1.1884, 1.0998]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.csc(|[1, 2])} $\to$ \texttt{|[1.1884, 1.0998]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.csc(\{1, 2\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{1.0998, 1.1884\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.csc(1.570796327)      #c# Returns 1.0 (pi/2)
f.trig.csc(1.0)              #c# Returns approximately 1.1884
f.trig.csc(3.141592654)      #c# Returns inf (sin = 0)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Degree Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.csc(90, unit="deg")    #c# Returns 1.0
f.trig.csc(30, unit="deg")    #c# Returns 2.0
f.trig.csc(0, unit="deg")     #c# Returns inf (sin = 0)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.csc(1 + 2j)           #c# Returns approximately 0.0998 - 0.2033j
f.trig.csc(0 + 1j)           #c# Returns approximately -0.8509j
f.trig.csc(1 + 0j)           #c# Returns approximately 1.1884 + 0j
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.csc(|[1.5708, 1.0])           #c# Returns |[1.0, 1.1884]
f.trig.csc(|[90, 30], unit="deg")    #c# Returns |[1.0, 2.0]
f.trig.csc(|[[1.5708, 1.0]])         #c# Returns |[[1.0, 1.1884]]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}
Numeric Lists are automatically converted to Tensors:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.csc([1.5708, 1.0])          #c# Returns |[1.0, 1.1884]
f.trig.csc([90, 30], unit="deg")   #c# Returns |[1.0, 2.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Mixed Unit Examples}
When \texttt{unit} is a list, each element uses its corresponding unit:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.csc([90, 1.5708], unit=["deg", "rad"]) #c# Returns |[1.0, 1.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
Sets are processed element-wise. Duplicates may result from csc:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.csc({1.5708, 1.0})       #c# Returns {1.0, 1.1884}
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.csc([])                    #c# Value Error (empty list)
f.trig.csc([1, "two"])            #c# Type Error (non-numeric)
f.trig.csc([1, 2], 3, 4)          #c# Type Error (too many arguments)
f.trig.csc(1, unit="invalid")     #c# Value Error (invalid unit)
f.trig.csc([1, 2], unit=["deg"])  #c# Runtime Error (unit shape mismatch)
f.trig.csc(3.141592654)           #c# Division by Zero Error (sin = 0)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Versine Functions}
Historical trigonometric functions used in navigation, astronomy, and surveying. All support \texttt{unit="deg"} and tensors.

\paragraph{Versine}
The \texttt{versin} function computes the versed sine: $1 - \cos(x)$.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.versin", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from versin.
\item Range: $[0, 2]$ for real input. Period: $2\pi$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\operatorname{versin}(x) = 1 - \cos(x) = 2\sin^2\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\operatorname{versin}_{\text{deg}}(x) = \operatorname{versin}\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Even function: $\operatorname{versin}(-x) = \operatorname{versin}(x)$
\item Periodicity: $\operatorname{versin}(x + 2\pi) = \operatorname{versin}(x)$
\item Relation to vercos: $\operatorname{versin}(x) + \operatorname{vercos}(x) = 2$
\item Alternative form: $\operatorname{versin}(x) = 2\sin^2(x/2)$
\item Range: $[0, 2]$ for real $x$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.versin(0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.0} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.versin(0.0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.0} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.versin(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{2.1668+3.2985j} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.versin([0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 0.4597]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.versin(|[0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 0.4597]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.versin(\{0, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0.0, 0.4597\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.versin(0)              #c# Returns 0.0
f.trig.versin(3.141592654)    #c# Returns 2.0
f.trig.versin(1.570796327)    #c# Returns 1.0
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Degree Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.versin(0, unit="deg")     #c# Returns 0.0
f.trig.versin(180, unit="deg")   #c# Returns 2.0
f.trig.versin(60, unit="deg")    #c# Returns 0.5
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.versin(1 + 2j)        #c# Returns approximately 2.1668 + 3.2985j
f.trig.versin(0 + 1j)        #c# Returns approximately -1.3818
f.trig.versin(1 + 0j)        #c# Returns approximately 0.4597 + 0j
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.versin(|[0, 1.5708, 3.1416])      #c# Returns |[0.0, 1.0, 2.0]
f.trig.versin(|[0, 60, 180], unit="deg") #c# Returns |[0.0, 0.5, 2.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.versin([0, 1.5708, 3.1416])     #c# Returns |[0.0, 1.0, 2.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Mixed Unit Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.versin([0, 0], unit=["deg", "rad"])    #c# Returns |[0.0, 0.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.versin({0, 3.141592654})  #c# Returns {0.0, 2.0}
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.versin([])                    #c# Value Error (empty list)
f.trig.versin([1, "two"])            #c# Type Error (non-numeric)
f.trig.versin([1, 2], 3, 4)          #c# Type Error (too many arguments)
f.trig.versin(1, unit="invalid")     #c# Value Error (invalid unit)
f.trig.versin([1, 2], unit=["deg"])  #c# Runtime Error (unit shape mismatch)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Vercosine}
The \texttt{vercos} function computes the versed cosine: $1 + \cos(x)$.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.vercos", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from vercos.
\item Range: $[0, 2]$ for real input. Period: $2\pi$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\operatorname{vercos}(x) = 1 + \cos(x) = 2\cos^2\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\operatorname{vercos}_{\text{deg}}(x) = \operatorname{vercos}\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Even function: $\operatorname{vercos}(-x) = \operatorname{vercos}(x)$
\item Periodicity: $\operatorname{vercos}(x + 2\pi) = \operatorname{vercos}(x)$
\item Relation to versin: $\operatorname{versin}(x) + \operatorname{vercos}(x) = 2$
\item Alternative form: $\operatorname{vercos}(x) = 2\cos^2(x/2)$
\item Range: $[0, 2]$ for real $x$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.vercos(0)} $\to$ \texttt{2.0} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.vercos(0.0)} $\to$ \texttt{2.0} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.vercos(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{0.1248+3.0519j} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.vercos([0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[2.0, 1.5403]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.vercos(|[0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[2.0, 1.5403]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.vercos(\{0, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{1.5403, 2.0\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}

\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Degree Examples}

f.trig.vercos(180, unit="deg")   #c# Returns 0.0
f.trig.vercos(60, unit="deg")    #c# Returns 1.5
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}

\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}

\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Mixed Unit Examples}
