\paragraph{Coversine}
The \texttt{coversin} function computes the coversed sine: $1 - \sin(x)$.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.coversin", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from coversin.
\item Range: $[0, 2]$ for real input. Period: $2\pi$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\operatorname{coversin}(x) = 1 - \sin(x) = 2\sin^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{x}{2}\right)
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\operatorname{coversin}_{\text{deg}}(x) = \operatorname{coversin}\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Periodicity: $\operatorname{coversin}(x + 2\pi) = \operatorname{coversin}(x)$
\item Relation to covercos: $\operatorname{coversin}(x) + \operatorname{covercos}(x) = 2$
\item Alternative form: $\operatorname{coversin}(x) = 2\sin^2(\pi/4 - x/2)$
\item Range: $[0, 2]$ for real $x$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\end{center}

\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Covercosine}
The \texttt{covercos} function computes the coversed cosine: $1 + \sin(x)$.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.covercos", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from covercos.
\item Range: $[0, 2]$ for real input. Period: $2\pi$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\operatorname{covercos}(x) = 1 + \sin(x) = 2\cos^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{x}{2}\right)
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\operatorname{covercos}_{\text{deg}}(x) = \operatorname{covercos}\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Periodicity: $\operatorname{covercos}(x + 2\pi) = \operatorname{covercos}(x)$
\item Relation to coversin: $\operatorname{coversin}(x) + \operatorname{covercos}(x) = 2$
\item Alternative form: $\operatorname{covercos}(x) = 2\cos^2(\pi/4 - x/2)$
\item Range: $[0, 2]$ for real $x$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.covercos(0)} $\to$ \texttt{1.0} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.covercos(0.0)} $\to$ \texttt{1.0} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.covercos(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{0.1248+3.2985j} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.covercos([0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[1.0, 1.8415]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.covercos(|[0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[1.0, 1.8415]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.covercos(\{0, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{1.0, 1.8415\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

f.trig.covercos(1.570796327)    #c# Returns 2.0
f.trig.covercos(3.141592654)    #c# Returns 1.0
\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Haversine}
The \texttt{havers} function computes the half versine: $(1 - \cos(x))/2$. Historically crucial in navigation (haversine formula for great-circle distance).\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.havers", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from havers.
\item Range: $[0, 1]$ for real input. Period: $2\pi$.
\item Inverse: $\text{hav}^{-1}(y) = 2\arcsin(\sqrt{y})$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\operatorname{havers}(x) = \frac{1 - \cos(x)}{2} = \sin^2\left(\frac{x}{2}\right)
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\operatorname{havers}_{\text{deg}}(x) = \operatorname{havers}\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Even function: $\operatorname{havers}(-x) = \operatorname{havers}(x)$
\item Periodicity: $\operatorname{havers}(x + 2\pi) = \operatorname{havers}(x)$
\item Relation to havercos: $\operatorname{havers}(x) + \operatorname{havercos}(x) = 1$
\item Alternative form: $\operatorname{havers}(x) = \sin^2(x/2)$
\item Inverse: $\operatorname{hav}^{-1}(y) = 2\arcsin(\sqrt{y})$
\item Range: $[0, 1]$ for real $x$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.havers(0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.0} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.havers(0.0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.0} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.havers(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{0.7232+0.8207j} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.havers([0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 0.2298]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.havers(|[0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 0.2298]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.havers(\{0, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0.0, 0.2298\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\operatorname{hacovercos}(x) = \frac{1 + \sin(x)}{2} = \cos^2\left(\frac{\pi}{4} - \frac{x}{2}\right)
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\operatorname{hacovercos}_{\text{deg}}(x) = \operatorname{hacovercos}\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Periodicity: $\operatorname{hacovercos}(x + 2\pi) = \operatorname{hacovercos}(x)$
\item Relation to hacoversin: $\operatorname{hacoversin}(x) + \operatorname{hacovercos}(x) = 1$
\item Alternative form: $\operatorname{hacovercos}(x) = \cos^2(\pi/4 - x/2)$
\item Range: $[0, 1]$ for real $x$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.hacovercos(0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.5} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.hacovercos(0.0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.5} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.hacovercos(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{-0.0834+1.5260j} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.hacovercos([0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.5, ...]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.hacovercos(|[0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.5, ...]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.hacovercos(\{0, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0.5, ...\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Exsecant}
The \texttt{exsec} function computes the external secant: $\sec(x) - 1$.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.exsec", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from exsec.
\item Range: $[0, \infty)$ for real input. Period: $2\pi$.
\item A Division by Zero Error is raised at $x = \pi/2 + n\pi$ (where $\cos(x) = 0$).
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\operatorname{exsec}(x) = \sec(x) - 1 = \frac{\operatorname{versin}(x)}{\cos(x)}
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\operatorname{exsec}_{\text{deg}}(x) = \operatorname{exsec}\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Even function: $\operatorname{exsec}(-x) = \operatorname{exsec}(x)$
\item Periodicity: $\operatorname{exsec}(x + 2\pi) = \operatorname{exsec}(x)$
\item Relation to sec: $\operatorname{exsec}(x) = \sec(x) - 1$
\item Alternative form: $\operatorname{exsec}(x) = \frac{2\sin^2(x/2)}{\cos(x)}$
\item Range: $[0, \infty)$ for real $x$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.exsec(0.0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.0} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.exsec(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{...} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.exsec([0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, ...]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.exsec(|[0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, ...]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.exsec(\{0, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0.0, ...\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\end{itemize}

\[
\operatorname{excsc}(x) = \csc(x) - 1 = \frac{\operatorname{coversin}(x)}{\sin(x)}
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}

\[
\operatorname{excsc}_{\text{deg}}(x) = \operatorname{excsc}\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)

\paragraph{Properties}

\paragraph{Chord}
The \texttt{crd} function computes the chord length subtending angle $x$: $2\sin(x/2)$.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.crd", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from crd.
\item Range: $[0, 2]$ for real input. Period: $2\pi$.
\item No singularities — defined for all real and complex $x$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\operatorname{crd}(x) = 2\sin\left(\frac{x}{2}\right) = \sqrt{2 \cdot \operatorname{versin}(x)}
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the argument is converted to radians:
\[
\operatorname{crd}_{\text{deg}}(x) = \operatorname{crd}\left(\frac{\pi}{180} \cdot x\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Odd function: $\operatorname{crd}(-x) = -\operatorname{crd}(x)$
\item Periodicity: $\operatorname{crd}(x + 2\pi) = \operatorname{crd}(x)$
\item Relation to versin: $\operatorname{crd}(x) = \sqrt{2 \cdot \operatorname{versin}(x)}$
\item Relation to sin: $\operatorname{crd}(x) = 2\sin(x/2)$
\item Range: $[0, 2]$ for real $x$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\subsubsection{Arcsine Function}
Computes the inverse sine. Angles are interpreted as radians by default.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.asin", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from asin.
\item Domain: $[-1, 1]$ for real input. Range: $[-\pi/2, \pi/2]$.
\item A Value Error is raised for $|x| > 1$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\arcsin(x) = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \frac{\binom{2n}{n}}{4^n (2n+1)} x^{2n+1}
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
For complex arguments $z = a + bi$:
\[
\arcsin(z) = -i \cdot \ln\left(iz + \sqrt{1 - z^2}\right)
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the result is converted to degrees:
\[
\arcsin_{\text{deg}}(x) = \frac{180}{\pi} \cdot \arcsin(x)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Odd function: $\arcsin(-x) = -\arcsin(x)$
\item Domain: $[-1, 1]$. Range: $[-\pi/2, \pi/2]$.
\item Inverse of sine: $\sin(\arcsin(x)) = x$ for $x \in [-1, 1]$.
\item Inverse identity: $\arcsin(\sin(x)) = x$ for $x \in [-\pi/2, \pi/2]$.
\item Derivative: $\frac{d}{dx}\arcsin(x) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}$
\item Complementary: $\arcsin(x) + \arccos(x) = \pi/2$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.asin(0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.0} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.asin(0.5)} $\to$ \texttt{0.5236} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.asin(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{...} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.asin([0, 0.5])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 0.5236]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.asin(|[0, 0.5])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 0.5236]} \\

\end{center}

\subsubsection{Arccosine Function}
Computes the inverse cosine. Angles are interpreted as radians by default.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.acos", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from acos.
\item Domain: $[-1, 1]$ for real input. Range: $[0, \pi]$.
\item A Value Error is raised for $|x| > 1$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\arccos(x) = \frac{\pi}{2} - \arcsin(x) = \frac{\pi}{2} - \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \frac{\binom{2n}{n}}{4^n (2n+1)} x^{2n+1}
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
For complex arguments $z = a + bi$:
\[
\arccos(z) = -i \cdot \ln\left(z + \sqrt{z^2 - 1}\right)
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the result is converted to degrees:
\[
\arccos_{\text{deg}}(x) = \frac{180}{\pi} \cdot \arccos(x)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Symmetry: $\arccos(-x) = \pi - \arccos(x)$
\item Domain: $[-1, 1]$. Range: $[0, \pi]$.
\item Inverse of cosine: $\cos(\arccos(x)) = x$ for $x \in [-1, 1]$.
\item Inverse identity: $\arccos(\cos(x)) = x$ for $x \in [0, \pi]$.
\item Derivative: $\frac{d}{dx}\arccos(x) = -\frac{1}{\sqrt{1 - x^2}}$
\item Complementary: $\arccos(x) = \pi/2 - \arcsin(x)$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.acos(1)} $\to$ \texttt{0.0} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.acos(0.5)} $\to$ \texttt{1.0472} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.acos(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{...} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.acos([0, 0.5])} $\to$ \texttt{|[1.5708, 1.0472]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.acos(|[0, 0.5])} $\to$ \texttt{|[1.5708, 1.0472]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.acos(\{0, 0.5\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0.5, 1.0472\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

f.trig.acos(1)           #c# Returns 0.0
f.trig.acos(-0.5)        #c# Returns approximately 2.0944 (2pi/3)
f.trig.acos(-1)          #c# Returns approximately 3.1416 (pi)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Degree Examples}

\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Arctangent Function}
Computes the inverse tangent. Angles are interpreted as radians by default.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.atan", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from atan.
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$. Range: $(-\pi/2, \pi/2)$.
\item No domain restrictions.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\arctan(x) = \sum_{n=0}^{\infty} \frac{(-1)^n x^{2n+1}}{2n+1}
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
For complex arguments $z = a + bi$:
\[
\arctan(z) = \frac{i}{2} \cdot \ln\left(\frac{i + z}{i - z}\right)
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the result is converted to degrees:
\[
\arctan_{\text{deg}}(x) = \frac{180}{\pi} \cdot \arctan(x)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Odd function: $\arctan(-x) = -\arctan(x)$
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$. Range: $(-\pi/2, \pi/2)$.
\item Inverse of tangent: $\tan(\arctan(x)) = x$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$.
\item Inverse identity: $\arctan(\tan(x)) = x$ for $x \in (-\pi/2, \pi/2)$.
\item Derivative: $\frac{d}{dx}\arctan(x) = \frac{1}{1 + x^2}$
\item Reciprocal: $\arctan(x) + \arctan(1/x) = \pi/2$ for $x > 0$.
\item Limits: $\arctan(+\infty) = \pi/2$, $\arctan(-\infty) = -\pi/2$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.atan(0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.0} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.atan(1.0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.7854} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.atan(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{...} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.atan([0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 0.7854]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.atan(|[0, 1])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 0.7854]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.atan(\{0, 1\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0.0, 0.7854\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}

f.trig.atan(1)           #c# Returns approximately 0.7854 (pi/4)
f.trig.atan(-1)          #c# Returns approximately -0.7854
f.trig.atan(inf)         #c# Returns approximately 1.5708 (pi/2)
f.trig.atan(-inf)        #c# Returns approximately -1.5708
\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}

\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Mixed Unit Examples}

\subsubsection{Arccotangent Function}
Computes the inverse cotangent. Angles are interpreted as radians by default.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.acot", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from acot.
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$. Range: $(0, \pi)$.
\item No domain restrictions.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\operatorname{arccot}(x) = \frac{\pi}{2} - \arctan(x)
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
For complex arguments $z = a + bi$:
\[
\operatorname{arccot}(z) = \frac{i}{2} \cdot \ln\left(\frac{z - i}{z + i}\right)
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the result is converted to degrees:
\[
\operatorname{arccot}_{\text{deg}}(x) = \frac{180}{\pi} \cdot \operatorname{arccot}(x)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Antisymmetry: $\operatorname{arccot}(-x) = \pi - \operatorname{arccot}(x)$
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$. Range: $(0, \pi)$.
\item Inverse of cot: $\cot(\operatorname{arccot}(x)) = x$ for all $x \in \mathbb{R}$.
\item Inverse identity: $\operatorname{arccot}(\cot(x)) = x$ for $x \in (0, \pi)$.
\item Derivative: $\frac{d}{dx}\operatorname{arccot}(x) = -\frac{1}{1 + x^2}$
\item Relation to atan: $\operatorname{arccot}(x) = \pi/2 - \arctan(x)$.
\item Limits: $\operatorname{arccot}(+\infty) = 0$, $\operatorname{arccot}(-\infty) = \pi$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.acot(1)} $\to$ \texttt{0.7854} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.acot(1.0)} $\to$ \texttt{0.7854} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.acot(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{...} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.acot([1, inf])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.7854, 0.0]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.acot(|[1, inf])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.7854, 0.0]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.acot(\{1, inf\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0.0, 0.7854\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

f.trig.acot(-1)          #c# Returns approximately 2.3562 (3pi/4)

\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}

\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Mixed Unit Examples}

\subsubsection{Arcsecant Function}
Computes the inverse secant. Angles are interpreted as radians by default.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.asec", "(", arg, [",", "unit", "=", unit], ")"
arg  =  int | real | complex | tensor | list | set
unit =  string | tensor | list
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, \texttt{tensor}, \texttt{list}, or \texttt{set}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: string (default \texttt{"rad"}), \texttt{tensor}, or \texttt{list}.
\item If \texttt{unit} is a tensor or list, it must match the shape of \texttt{arg}; otherwise a Runtime Error is raised.
\item Sets cannot be used as \texttt{unit} because they lack structure (no indexing, no shape).
\item If \texttt{arg} is a \texttt{set}, \texttt{unit} must be a single value (scalar).
\item Numeric lists are automatically converted to tensors.
\item Sets are processed element-wise; duplicates may result from asec.
\item Domain: $(-\infty, -1] \cup [1, \infty)$ for real input. Range: $[0, \pi/2) \cup (\pi/2, \pi]$.
\item A Value Error is raised for $|x| < 1$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\operatorname{arcsec}(x) = \arccos\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)
\]

\paragraph{Complex Definition}
For complex arguments $z = a + bi$:
\[
\operatorname{arcsec}(z) = \arccos\left(\frac{1}{z}\right)
\]

\paragraph{Degree Conversion}
When \texttt{unit="deg"}, the result is converted to degrees:
\[
\operatorname{arcsec}_{\text{deg}}(x) = \frac{180}{\pi} \cdot \operatorname{arcsec}(x)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item Symmetry: $\operatorname{arcsec}(-x) = \pi - \operatorname{arcsec}(x)$
\item Domain: $(-\infty, -1] \cup [1, \infty)$. Range: $[0, \pi/2) \cup (\pi/2, \pi]$.
\item Inverse of sec: $\sec(\operatorname{arcsec}(x)) = x$ for $|x| \geq 1$.
\item Inverse identity: $\operatorname{arcsec}(\sec(x)) = x$ for $x \in [0, \pi/2) \cup (\pi/2, \pi]$.
\item Derivative: $\frac{d}{dx}\operatorname{arcsec}(x) = \frac{1}{|x|\sqrt{x^2 - 1}}$
\item Special values: $\operatorname{arcsec}(1) = 0$, $\operatorname{arcsec}(-1) = \pi$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Return Type}
The Return Type is determined by the Input Type:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} or \texttt{real} input: returns \texttt{real}
\item \texttt{complex} input: returns \texttt{complex}
\item Tensor/list input: returns \texttt{tensor}
\item Set input: returns \texttt{set}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Type Behavior}
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\textbf{Input Type} & \textbf{Output Type} & \textbf{Example} \\ \hline
\texttt{int} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.asec(1)} $\to$ \texttt{0.0} \\
\texttt{real} & \texttt{real} & \texttt{f.trig.asec(2.0)} $\to$ \texttt{1.0472} \\
\texttt{complex} & \texttt{complex} & \texttt{f.trig.asec(1+2j)} $\to$ \texttt{...} \\
\texttt{list} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.asec([1, 2])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 1.0472]} \\
\texttt{tensor} & \texttt{tensor} & \texttt{f.trig.asec(|[1, 2])} $\to$ \texttt{|[0.0, 1.0472]} \\
\texttt{set} & \texttt{set} & \texttt{f.trig.asec(\{1, 2\})} $\to$ \texttt{\{0.0, 1.0472\}} \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\paragraph{Scalar Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.asec(1)           #c# Returns 0.0
f.trig.asec(-1)          #c# Returns approximately 3.1416 (pi)
f.trig.asec(2)           #c# Returns approximately 1.0472 (pi/3)
f.trig.asec(-2)          #c# Returns approximately 2.0944 (2pi/3)
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Degree Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.asec(1, unit="deg")     #c# Returns 0.0
f.trig.asec(2, unit="deg")     #c# Returns 60.0
f.trig.asec(-2, unit="deg")    #c# Returns 120.0
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Complex Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.asec(1 + 2j)        #c# Returns approximately ...
f.trig.asec(0 + 1j)        #c# Returns approximately ...
f.trig.asec(1 + 0j)        #c# Returns approximately 0.0 + 0j
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Tensor Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.asec(|[1, 2, -2])            #c# Returns |[0.0, 1.0472, 2.0944]
f.trig.asec(|[1, 2, -2], unit="deg") #c# Returns |[0.0, 60.0, 120.0]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{List Auto-conversion}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.asec([1, 2, -2])            #c# Returns |[0.0, 1.0472, 2.0944]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Mixed Unit Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.asec([1, 2], unit=["deg", "rad"])  #c# Returns |[0.0, 1.0472]
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Set Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.asec({1, 2, -2})  #c# Returns {0.0, 1.0472, 2.0944}
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.asec(0.5)                   #c# Value Error (|x| < 1)
f.trig.asec(-0.5)                  #c# Value Error (|x| < 1)
f.trig.asec([])                    #c# Value Error (empty list)
f.trig.asec([1, "two"])            #c# Type Error (non-numeric)
f.trig.asec([1, 2], 3, 4)         #c# Type Error (too many arguments)
f.trig.asec(1, unit="invalid")     #c# Value Error (invalid unit)
f.trig.asec([1, 2], unit=["deg"])  #c# Runtime Error (unit shape mismatch)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Arccosecant Function}
Computes the inverse cosecant $\text{acsc}(x) = \text{asin}(1/x)$. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:

\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\item Optional \texttt{unit}: \texttt{"rad"} (default) or \texttt{"deg"}.
\item Result: \texttt{real}.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised for $|x| < 1$ (out of domain).
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.acsc(1)                        #c# Returns approximately 1.5708 (pi/2)
f.trig.acsc(2)                        #c# Returns approximately 0.5236 (pi/6)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Hyperbolic Sine Function}
Computes the hyperbolic sine of the argument. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}

\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not a numeric value or list of numeric values.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.hyp.sinh(0)            #c# Returns 0.0
f.hyp.sinh(1.443635475)  #c# Returns approximately 2.0
f.hyp.sinh(1 + 2j)       #c# Returns approximately -0.4891 + 0.9889j
\end{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}

\item Result: \texttt{real} for \texttt{int}/\texttt{real} input, \texttt{complex} for \texttt{complex} input.
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$ and all $\mathbb{C}$. No restrictions.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not a numeric value or list of numeric values.
\end{itemize}

\begin{verbatim}
f.hyp.cosh(0)          #c# Returns 1.0
f.hyp.cosh(1)          #c# Returns approximately 1.5431
f.hyp.cosh(1 + 2j)     #c# Returns approximately -0.4891 + 0.9889j
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Hyperbolic Tangent Function}
Computes the hyperbolic tangent of the argument. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.hyp.tanh", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, or list of numeric values.
\item Result: \texttt{real} for \texttt{int}/\texttt{real} input, \texttt{complex} for \texttt{complex} input.
\item Domain for real: all $\mathbb{R}$. For complex: all $\mathbb{C}$ where $\cosh z \neq 0$.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not a numeric value or list of numeric values.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.hyp.tanh(0)          #c# Returns 0.0
f.hyp.tanh(1)          #c# Returns approximately 0.7616
f.hyp.tanh(0 + 1j)     #c# Returns approximately 0.0 + 0.5524j
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Hyperbolic Cotangent Function}
Computes the hyperbolic cotangent $\coth(x) = \frac{\cosh(x)}{\sinh(x)}$. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.hyp.coth", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, or \texttt{complex}.
\item Result: same type as input.
\item A \texttt{Division by Zero Error} is raised at $x = 0$.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.hyp.coth(1)          #c# Returns approximately 1.3130
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Hyperbolic Secant Function}
Computes the hyperbolic secant $\text{sech}(x) = \frac{1}{\cosh(x)}$. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.hyp.sech", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, or \texttt{complex}.
\item Result: same type as input.
\item No domain restrictions.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.hyp.sech(0)          #c# Returns 1.0
f.hyp.sech(1)          #c# Returns approximately 0.6481
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Hyperbolic Cosecant Function}
Computes the hyperbolic cosecant $\text{csch}(x) = \frac{1}{\sinh(x)}$. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.hyp.csch", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, or \texttt{complex}.
\item Result: same type as input.
\item A \texttt{Division by Zero Error} is raised at $x = 0$.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.hyp.csch(1)          #c# Returns approximately 0.8509
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Complex Hyperbolic Formulas}
For complex $z = a + bi$, the hyperbolic functions are computed as:
\[
\sinh(a + bi) = \sinh(a)\cos(b) + i\cosh(a)\sin(b)
\]
\[
\cosh(a + bi) = \cosh(a)\cos(b) + i\sinh(a)\sin(b)
\]
\[
\tanh(a + bi) = \frac{\sinh(a + bi)}{\cosh(a + bi)}
\]
Equivalently: $\sinh(z) = -i \sin(iz)$, $\cosh(z) = \cos(iz)$, $\tanh(z) = \sinh(z) / \cosh(z)$.

\item Result: \texttt{real} for \texttt{int}/\texttt{real} input, \texttt{complex} for \texttt{complex} input.
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$ and all $\mathbb{C}$. No restrictions.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not a numeric value or list of numeric values.
\end{itemize}

f.hyp.asinh(0)          #c# Returns 0.0
f.hyp.asinh(1)          #c# Returns approximately 0.8814
f.hyp.asinh(1 + 2j)     #c# Returns approximately 1.4694 + 1.0987j
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Hyperbolic Arccosine Function}

\item Domain for real: $[1, \infty)$. A \texttt{Value Error} is raised for $x < 1$.
\item Domain for complex: all $\mathbb{C}$ (using principal branch).
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not a numeric value or list of numeric values.
\end{itemize}

f.hyp.acosh(1)          #c# Returns 0.0
f.hyp.acosh(2)          #c# Returns approximately 1.3170

\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Hyperbolic Arctangent Function}
Computes the inverse hyperbolic tangent. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}

\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, or list of numeric values.
\item Result: \texttt{real} for \texttt{int}/\texttt{real} input, \texttt{complex} for \texttt{complex} input.
\item Domain for real: $(-1, 1)$. A \texttt{Value Error} is raised for $|x| \geq 1$.
\item Domain for complex: all $\mathbb{C}$ where $z \neq \pm 1$.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not a numeric value or list of numeric values.
\end{itemize}

f.hyp.atanh(0)          #c# Returns 0.0
f.hyp.atanh(0.5)        #c# Returns approximately 0.5493

\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Inverse Hyperbolic Cotangent Function}
Computes $\text{acoth}(x) = \frac{1}{2} \ln\!\left(\frac{x+1}{x-1}\right)$. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.hyp.acoth", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, or \texttt{complex}.
\item Result: same type as input.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised for real $|x| \leq 1$ (out of domain).
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.hyp.acoth(2)         #c# Returns approximately 0.5493
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Inverse Hyperbolic Secant Function}
Computes $\text{asech}(x) = \text{acosh}(1/x)$. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.hyp.asech", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, or \texttt{complex}.
\item Result: same type as input.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised for real $x \leq 0$ or $x > 1$ (out of domain).
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.hyp.asech(1)         #c# Returns 0.0
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Inverse Hyperbolic Cosecant Function}
Computes $\text{acsch}(x) = \text{asch}(1/x)$. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.hyp.acsch", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, or \texttt{complex}.
\item Result: same type as input.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised at $x = 0$ (singularity).
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.hyp.acsch(1)         #c# Returns approximately 0.8814
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Complex Hyperbolic Inverse Formulas}
For complex arguments, the inverse hyperbolic functions are computed via the complex logarithm:
\[
\operatorname{asinh}(z) = \ln\!\left(z + \sqrt{z^2 + 1}\right)
\]
\[
\operatorname{acosh}(z) = \ln\!\left(z + \sqrt{z^2 - 1}\right)
\]
\[
\operatorname{atanh}(z) = \frac{1}{2} \ln\!\left(\frac{1 + z}{1 - z}\right)
\]

\subsubsection{Gudermannian Function}
The Gudermannian function bridges hyperbolic and circular functions without complex numbers. It maps the hyperbolic angle (rapidity) to the circular angle.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.hyp.gd", "(", ( int | real ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\item Result: \texttt{real} (circular angle in radians).
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\text{gd}(x) = 2 \arctan(e^x) - \frac{\pi}{2} = \arcsin(\tanh(x)) = \arctan(\sinh(x))
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item $\text{gd}(0) = 0$
\item $\text{gd}(x) \to \pi/2$ as $x \to +\infty$
\item $\text{gd}(x) \to -\pi/2$ as $x \to -\infty$
\item Odd function: $\text{gd}(-x) = -\text{gd}(x)$
\item Derivative: $\text{gd}'(x) = \text{sech}(x)$
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{itemize}
\item $x = 0$: returns $0$
\item Large $|x| > 150$: clamped to $\pm\pi/2$
\end{itemize}

Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.hyp.gd(0)         #c# Returns 0.0
f.hyp.gd(1)         #c# Returns approximately 0.8658 (49.6°)
f.hyp.gd(-1)        #c# Returns approximately -0.8658
f.hyp.gd(2)         #c# Returns approximately 1.3018 (74.6°)
f.hyp.gd(100)       #c# Returns approximately pi/2
f.hyp.gd(-100)      #c# Returns approximately -pi/2
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Inverse Gudermannian Function}
The inverse Gudermannian maps the circular angle back to the hyperbolic angle (rapidity).\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.hyp.gdInv", "(", ( int | real ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\item Result: \texttt{real} (hyperbolic angle / rapidity).
\item Domain: $(-\pi/2, \pi/2)$. A \texttt{Value Error} is raised for $|x| \geq \pi/2$.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Definition}
\[
\text{gd}^{-1}(x) = \ln(\sec(x) + \tan(x)) = \text{arsinh}(\tan(x)) = \ln\left(\tan\left(\frac{x}{2} + \frac{\pi}{4}\right)\right)
\]

\paragraph{Properties}
\begin{itemize}
\item $\text{gd}^{-1}(0) = 0$
\item $\text{gd}^{-1}(x) \to +\infty$ as $x \to \pi/2$
\item $\text{gd}^{-1}(x) \to -\infty$ as $x \to -\pi/2$
\item Odd function: $\text{gd}^{-1}(-x) = -\text{gd}^{-1}(x)$
\item Derivative: $\frac{d}{dx}\text{gd}^{-1}(x) = \sec(x)$
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Edge Cases}
\begin{itemize}
\item $x = 0$: returns $0$
\item $|x| \geq \pi/2$: raises \texttt{Value Error} (out of domain)
\end{itemize}

Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.hyp.gdInv(0)          #c# Returns 0.0
f.hyp.gdInv(0.8658)     #c# Returns approximately 1.0
f.hyp.gdInv(1.3018)     #c# Returns approximately 2.0
f.hyp.gdInv(pi/4)       #c# Returns approximately 0.8814
f.hyp.gdInv(pi/2)       #c# Value Error (out of domain)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Roundtrip Property}
The Gudermannian and its inverse are true inverses:
\[
\text{gd}(\text{gd}^{-1}(x)) = x \quad \text{for } x \in (-\pi/2, \pi/2)
\]
\[
\text{gd}^{-1}(\text{gd}(x)) = x \quad \text{for all } x \in \mathbb{R}
\]

\subsubsection{Exponential Function}
Returns $e$ raised to the power of the argument. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.log.exp", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, or list of numeric values.
\item Result: \texttt{real} for \texttt{int}/\texttt{real} input, \texttt{complex} for \texttt{complex} input.
\item Domain: all $\mathbb{R}$ and all $\mathbb{C}$.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not a numeric value or list of numeric values.
\end{itemize}
For complex $z = a + bi$, computed via Euler's Formula: $e^{a+bi} = e^a(\cos b + i \sin b)$.
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.log.exp(0)              #c# Returns 1.0
f.log.exp(1)              #c# Returns approximately 2.71828
f.log.exp(0 + 3.14159j)   #c# Returns approximately -1 + 0.0j (Euler's Identity)
f.log.exp(1 + 1j)         #c# Returns approximately 1.4687 + 2.2874j
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Square Root Function}
Returns the principal square root of the argument. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.log.sqrt", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}, or list of numeric values.
\item Result: \texttt{real} for non-negative \texttt{int}/\texttt{real} input, \texttt{complex} for negative real or \texttt{complex} input.
\item Domain for real: $[0, \infty)$ returns \texttt{real}. Negative real input returns \texttt{complex} (principal branch).
\item Domain for complex: all $\mathbb{C}$, principal branch $-\pi < \arg(\sqrt{z}) \leq \pi$.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not a numeric value or list of numeric values.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.log.sqrt(9)          #c# Returns 3.0
f.log.sqrt(2)          #c# Returns approximately 1.41421
f.log.sqrt(-1)         #c# Returns 0 + 1j
f.log.sqrt(-4)         #c# Returns 0 + 2j
f.log.sqrt(3 + 4j)     #c# Returns approximately 2 + 1j
f.log.sqrt(0)          #c# Returns 0.0
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Power Function}
Computes exponentiation and optional modular exponentiation. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.log.pow", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ",", ( int | real | complex ), [ ",", int ], ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{f.log.pow(a, b)}: returns $a^b$. Both arguments \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, or \texttt{complex}.
\item \texttt{f.log.pow(a, b, c)}: returns $a^b \bmod c$ (modular exponentiation). \texttt{a}, \texttt{c} must be \texttt{int}, \texttt{c} must be $> 0$, \texttt{b} can be negative (computes modular inverse).
\item Return type: \texttt{int} when both arguments are \texttt{int} and the result is exact; \texttt{real} otherwise.
\item Without \texttt{c}: negative exponents on \texttt{int} produce \texttt{real}.
\item With \texttt{c}: negative \texttt{b} computes $a^b \bmod c$ via modular multiplicative inverse (exists when $\gcd(a, c) = 1$).
\item For complex $z_1, z_2$: $z_1^{z_2} = e^{z_2 \ln z_1}$ (principal branch). $0^z$ only defined for $\operatorname{Re}(z) > 0$.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if arguments are not numeric.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.log.pow(2, 3)         #c# Returns 8 (int)
f.log.pow(9, 0.5)       #c# Returns 3.0 (real)
f.log.pow(3, 4, 7)      #c# Returns 4  (81 mod 7)
f.log.pow(2, 32, 100)   #c# Returns 96  (4294967296 mod 100)
f.log.pow(3, -1, 7)     #c# Returns 5  (modular inverse: 3*5=15==1 mod 7)
f.log.pow(2, 1 + 1j)    #c# Returns approximately 1.538 + 0.999j
f.log.pow(1 + 1j, 2)    #c# Returns 0 + 2j
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Hyperoperation}
Computes the nth hyperoperation: addition, multiplication, exponentiation, tetration, pentation, or hexation. Optionally applies modular reduction.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.log.hyperop", "(", int, ",", ( int | real | complex ), ",", ( int | real | complex ),
                    [ ",", int ], ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{n} (int): hyperoperation level (0--6).
\item \texttt{a}: left operand. Any numeric type.
\item \texttt{b}: right operand. Any numeric type for n $\leq$ 3; must be \texttt{int} for n $\geq$ 4.
\item \texttt{m} (int, optional): modulus. Must be $> 0$. When present, each intermediate step is reduced mod \texttt{m}.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Levels:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{cll}
n & Name & Definition \\ \hline
0 & Successor & $a + 1$ (b ignored) \\
1 & Addition & $a + b$ \\
2 & Multiplication & $a \times b$ \\
3 & Exponentiation & $a^b$ \\
4 & Tetration & ${}^b a$ (iterated exponentiation) \\
5 & Pentation & (iterated tetration) \\
6 & Hexation & (iterated pentation) \\
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{int} $\times$ \texttt{int} returns \texttt{int} (arbitrary precision). With modulus, result is in $[0, m)$.
\item Any \texttt{real} operand returns \texttt{real}.
\item Any \texttt{complex} operand returns \texttt{complex}. Modulus not supported for complex operands.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if \texttt{n} is not \texttt{int}, or if n $\geq$ 4 and \texttt{b} is not \texttt{int}.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if n $<$ 0 or n $>$ 6.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if \texttt{m} is provided and is not \texttt{int}, or if \texttt{a} is \texttt{complex}.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if n $\geq$ 3 and $a = 0$ and $b = 0$.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if n $\geq$ 4 and $a = 0$ and $b \leq 0$.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if n $\geq$ 4 and $b < 0$.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if \texttt{m} $\leq 0$.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if the result exceeds $2^{20}$ bits (only when no modulus is specified).
\end{itemize}

Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.log.hyperop(0, 5, 99)           #c# Returns 6          (successor, b ignored)
f.log.hyperop(1, 3, 4)            #c# Returns 7          (addition)
f.log.hyperop(2, 3, 4)            #c# Returns 12         (multiplication)
f.log.hyperop(3, 2, 10)           #c# Returns 1024       (exponentiation)
f.log.hyperop(4, 2, 4)            #c# Returns 65536      (tetration: 2^2^2^2)
f.log.hyperop(4, 3, 3)            #c# Returns 7625597484987  (3^3^3)
f.log.hyperop(5, 2, 3)            #c# Returns 65536      (pentation)
f.log.hyperop(6, 2, 3)            #c# Returns 65536      (hexation)
f.log.hyperop(4, 1+1j, 2)         #c# Returns approximately 0 + 2j
f.log.hyperop(3, 2.5, 3)          #c# Returns 15.625     (real)

# With modulus
f.log.hyperop(3, 2, 10, 1000)     #c# Returns 24     (2^10 mod 1000 = 1024 mod 1000)
f.log.hyperop(4, 2, 8, 10000)     #c# Returns 5536   (2 ↑↑ 8 mod 10000)
f.log.hyperop(4, 3, 3, 100)       #c# Returns 87     (3^3^3 mod 100 = 7625597484987 mod 100)
f.log.hyperop(3, 7, 256, 256)     #c# Returns 0      (7^256 mod 256 = 0)
f.log.hyperop(2, 13, 17, 10)      #c# Returns 1      (13*17 mod 10 = 221 mod 10)
f.log.hyperop(4, 2, 4, 1)         #c# Returns 0      (anything mod 1 = 0)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Hyperroot}
Computes the hyperroot: the inverse of a hyperoperation. Finds $x$ such that $\text{hyperop}(n, x, b) = y$.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.log.nthhyprt", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ",", int, ",", int, ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{y}: the value to find the hyperroot of. Any numeric type.
\item \texttt{n} (int): hyperoperation level (3--6).
\item \texttt{b} (int): iteration count. Must be $\geq 1$.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Relation to \texttt{f.log.hyperop}:
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{f.log.nthhyprt(y, 3, b)} = $b$th root of $y$ = $y^{1/b}$ (inverse of exponentiation).
\item \texttt{f.log.nthhyprt(y, 4, b)} = $b$th hyperroot of $y$ (inverse of tetration).
\item \texttt{f.log.nthhyprt(y, 5, b)} = inverse of pentation.
\item \texttt{f.log.nthhyprt(y, 6, b)} = inverse of hexation.
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\item Returns \texttt{real} for \texttt{int}/\texttt{real} $y$; \texttt{complex} for \texttt{complex} $y$.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if \texttt{n} or \texttt{b} is not \texttt{int}, or if $n < 3$ or $n > 6$.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if $b < 1$.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if $y \leq 0$ and $n \geq 4$ (tetration base must be positive for real result).
\end{itemize}

Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.log.nthhyprt(27, 3, 3)             #c# Returns 3.0       (cube root of 27)
f.log.nthhyprt(65536, 4, 2)          #c# Returns 4          (2nd hyperroot: x^x = 65536 → x = 4)
f.log.nthhyprt(7625597484987, 4, 3)  #c# Returns 3  (3rd hyperroot: x^x^x = ...)
f.log.nthhyprt(16, 3, 4)             #c# Returns 2.0       (4th root of 16)
f.log.nthhyprt(0 + 64j, 3, 3)        #c# Returns approximately 2 + 2j  (complex root)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Super-Logarithm}
Computes the super-logarithm: the inverse of tetration. Finds $x$ such that $b \uparrow\uparrow x = y$. ``How many times must I tetrate $b$ to reach $y$?''\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.log.slog", "(", ( int | real ), ",", ( int | real ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{b} (int | real): the base of the tetration. Must be $> 0$.
\item \texttt{y} (int | real): the target value. Must be $\geq 1$ for real result.
\end{itemize}

\noindent Relation to \texttt{f.log.hyperop}:
\begin{itemize}
\item $\text{slog}(b, y) = x$ means $b \uparrow\uparrow x = y$.
\item Inverse of \texttt{f.log.hyperop(4, b, x)}.
\end{itemize}

\begin{itemize}
\item Returns \texttt{real}. Integer results are exact (e.g., $\text{slog}(2, 65536) = 4.0$ exactly).
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if arguments are not numeric.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if $b \leq 0$ or $y < 1$.
\end{itemize}

Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.log.slog(2, 1)             #c# Returns 0.0     (2 ↑↑ 0 = 1)
f.log.slog(2, 2)             #c# Returns 1.0     (2 ↑↑ 1 = 2)
f.log.slog(2, 4)             #c# Returns 2.0     (2 ↑↑ 2 = 2^2 = 4)
f.log.slog(2, 16)            #c# Returns 3.0     (2 ↑↑ 3 = 2^2^2 = 16)
f.log.slog(2, 65536)         #c# Returns 4.0     (2 ↑↑ 4 = 65536)
f.log.slog(3, 7625597484987) #c# Returns 3.0   (3 ↑↑ 3 = 3^3^3)
f.log.slog(2, 3)             #c# Returns approximately 1.409  (between 1 and 2)
f.log.slog(10, 1000)         #c# Returns approximately 2.388  (between 2 and 3)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Natural Logarithm}
Returns the natural logarithm (base $e$) of the argument. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.log.ln", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int} (must be $>$ 0), \texttt{real} (must be $>$ 0), \texttt{complex} ($\neq 0$).
\item Result: \texttt{real} for positive \texttt{int}/\texttt{real} input, \texttt{complex} for \texttt{complex} input.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised for non-positive real arguments.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not a numeric value or list of numeric values.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.log.ln(1)               #c# Returns 0.0
f.log.ln(c.math.e)        #c# Returns approximately 1.0
f.log.ln(100)             #c# Returns approximately 4.6052
f.log.ln(1 + 1j)          #c# Returns approximately 0.3466 + 0.7854j
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Base-10 Logarithm}
Returns the base-10 logarithm of the argument. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.log.log10", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int} (must be $>$ 0), \texttt{real} (must be $>$ 0), \texttt{complex} ($\neq 0$).
\item Result: \texttt{real} for positive \texttt{int}/\texttt{real} input, \texttt{complex} for \texttt{complex} input.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised for non-positive real arguments.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not a numeric value or list of numeric values.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.log10(1)          #c# Returns 0.0
f.log10(10)         #c# Returns 1.0
f.log10(1000)       #c# Returns 3.0
f.log10(1 + 1j)     #c# Returns approximately 0.1505 + 0.3411j
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Expm1 Function}
Computes $e^x - 1$ with high precision for small $x$, avoiding catastrophic cancellation. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.expm1", "(", ( int | real ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operand: \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\item Result: \texttt{real}.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.expm1(0)       #c# Returns 0.0
f.math.expm1(1)       #c# Returns approximately 1.7183 (e - 1)
f.math.expm1(0.001)   #c# Returns approximately 0.0010005  (more precise than exp(0.001) - 1)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Log1p Function}
Computes $\ln(1 + x)$ with high precision for small $x$, avoiding catastrophic cancellation. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.log1p", "(", ( int | real ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operand: \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\item Result: \texttt{real}.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised for $x \leq -1$.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.log1p(0)       #c# Returns 0.0
f.math.log1p(1)       #c# Returns approximately 0.6931 (ln 2)
f.math.log1p(0.001)   #c# Returns approximately 0.0009995  (more precise than ln(1.001))
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Exp2 Function}
Returns $2^x$. More precise and faster than \texttt{f.math.pow(2, x)}.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.exp2", "(", ( int | real ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operand: \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\item Result: \texttt{real}.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.exp2(0)       #c# Returns 1.0
f.math.exp2(1)       #c# Returns 2.0
f.math.exp2(10)      #c# Returns 1024.0
f.math.exp2(-1)      #c# Returns 0.5
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Logaddexp Function}
Returns $\ln(e^x + e^y)$, numerically stable. Prevents overflow when computing log of sum of exponentials.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.logaddexp", "(", real, ",", real, ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{real}, \texttt{real}.
\item Result: \texttt{real}.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.logaddexp(0, 0)      #c# Returns approximately 0.6931 (ln 2)
f.math.logaddexp(0, 1000)   #c# Returns approximately 1000.0 (no overflow)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Logaddexp2 Function}
Returns $\log_2(2^x + 2^y)$, numerically stable.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.logaddexp2", "(", real, ",", real, ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{real}, \texttt{real}.
\item Result: \texttt{real}.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.logaddexp2(0, 0)     #c# Returns 1.0 (log2(2))
f.math.logaddexp2(0, 1000)  #c# Returns approximately 1000.0 (no overflow)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{IsClose Function}
\label{sec:isclose}
Compares two scalar values for approximate equality using combined absolute and relative tolerance. This is the function form of the approximate equality operators (\texttt{\textasciitilde}, \texttt{\textasciitilde*}, \texttt{\textasciitilde+()}\textasciitilde, \texttt{\textasciitilde*()}\textasciitilde); unlike the operators, \texttt{f.math.isclose} can be passed as a function argument, used in lambdas, and has explicit tolerance parameters.

The comparison formula is:
\begin{verbatim}
|a - b| <= max(atol, rtol * max(|a|, |b|))
\end{verbatim}
The absolute tolerance \texttt{atol} dominates near zero (where relative tolerance would be meaningless). The relative tolerance \texttt{rtol} dominates for large values.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.isclose", "(", ( int | real ), ",", ( int | real ), [",", "rtol", "=", real], [",", "atol", "=", real], ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: two values to compare (\texttt{int} or \texttt{real}), optional \texttt{rtol} (relative tolerance, default 1e-9), optional \texttt{atol} (absolute tolerance, default 0.0).
\item Result: \texttt{boolean} (\texttt{True} if within tolerance, \texttt{False} otherwise).
\item The tolerance values must be non-negative. A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if \texttt{rtol < 0} or \texttt{atol < 0}.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Relationship to Operators}
\begin{itemize}
\item \texttt{f.math.isclose(a, b, atol=c)} is equivalent to \texttt{a \textasciitilde+(c)\textasciitilde b}
\item \texttt{f.math.isclose(a, b, rtol=c)} is equivalent to \texttt{a \textasciitilde*(c)\textasciitilde b}
\item \texttt{f.math.isclose(a, b)} uses \texttt{rtol=1e-9}, which is much tighter than the default \texttt{syst.flag.approx\_eps = 1}
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
                                            #c# Basic floating-point rounding
f.math.isclose(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3)              #c# Returns True  (0.1+0.2 = 0.30000000000000004)
f.math.isclose(1.0, 1.0)                    #c# Returns True
f.math.isclose(1.0, 2.0)                    #c# Returns False

                                            #c# Tolerance tuning
f.math.isclose(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3, rtol=0)      #c# Returns False  (strict: no relative tolerance)
f.math.isclose(1000.0, 1000.001)            #c# Returns False  (rtol=1e-9 too small)
f.math.isclose(1000.0, 1000.001, rtol=1e-4) #c# Returns True  (1e-4 covers 0.001/1000)

                                            #c# Absolute tolerance near zero
f.math.isclose(0.0, 1e-10)                  #c# Returns False  (atol=0, no absolute tolerance)
f.math.isclose(0.0, 1e-10, atol=1e-9)       #c# Returns True   (atol dominates near zero)

                                            #c# Composability
$f [func]= L::($x [real], $y [real]) -> { f.math.isclose($x, $y, rtol=1e-6) }
$f(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3)                          #c# Returns True  (usable in lambdas)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{AllClose Function}
\label{sec:allclose}
Compares two collections element-wise for approximate equality. Returns \texttt{True} if every corresponding pair of elements satisfies the tolerance check (same formula as \texttt{f.math.isclose}). Nested lists must have matching structure.

There is no operator equivalent for element-wise collection comparison.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.allclose", "(", list, ",", list, [",", "rtol", "=", real], [",", "atol", "=", real], ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: two \texttt{list}s of the same length (nested lists must have matching structure), optional \texttt{rtol} (relative tolerance, default 1e-9), optional \texttt{atol} (absolute tolerance, default 0.0).
\item Result: \texttt{boolean} (\texttt{True} if all element pairs are within tolerance).
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the operands are not both lists.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if the lists have different lengths.
\end{itemize}

\paragraph{Examples}
\begin{verbatim}
                                                                         #c# Basic usage
f.math.allclose(|[0.1, 0.2, 0.3], |[0.1+0.2, 0.2+0.1, 0.3+0.0])          #c# Returns True
f.math.allclose(|[1.0, 2.0], |[1.0, 2.0])                                #c# Returns True
f.math.allclose(|[1.0, 2.0], |[1.0, 2.1])                                #c# Returns False

                                                                         #c# Edge cases
f.math.allclose(|[], |[])                                                #c# Returns True  (empty lists)
f.math.allclose(|[0.0, 1e-10], |[0.0, 0.0], atol=1e-9)                   #c# Returns True

                                                                         #c# Nested lists
f.math.allclose(|[|1.0, 2.0], |3.0, 4.0]|], |[|1.0, 2.0], |3.0, 4.0]|])  #c# Returns True

                                                                         #c# Typical workflow: compare two filter responses
$response_a [tensor]= compute_response($coeffs_a, $coeffs_b)
$response_b [tensor]= compute_response($coeffs_c, $coeffs_d)
f.math.allclose($response_a, $response_b, rtol=1e-6)                     #c# Returns True if responses match
\end{verbatim}

\label{sec:complex_functions}

\subsubsection{Real Part Function}
Returns the real part of a complex number. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.cplx.real", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int} (promoted to \texttt{real}), \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}.
\item Result: \texttt{real}.
\item For \texttt{int}/\texttt{real} input, the value is returned as \texttt{real}.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised for non-numeric arguments.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.cplx.real(3 + 4j)   #c# Returns 3.0
f.cplx.real(-2j)      #c# Returns 0.0
f.cplx.real(5)        #c# Returns 5.0 (int promoted)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Imaginary Part Function}
Returns the imaginary part of a complex number. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.cplx.imag", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real} (imaginary part is 0.0), \texttt{complex}.
\item Result: \texttt{real}.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised for non-numeric arguments.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.cplx.imag(3 + 4j)   #c# Returns 4.0
f.cplx.imag(-2j)      #c# Returns -2.0
f.cplx.imag(5)        #c# Returns 0.0 (real has no imaginary part)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Angle Function}
Returns the angle (argument) of a complex number in radians, in the range $(-\pi, \pi]$. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.cplx.angle", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}.
\item Result: \texttt{real}.
\item \texttt{angle(0)} returns \texttt{0.0}.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised for non-numeric arguments.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.cplx.angle(1 + 0j)      #c# Returns 0.0
f.cplx.angle(0 + 1j)      #c# Returns 1.5708 (pi/2)
f.cplx.angle(-1 + 0j)     #c# Returns 3.14159 (pi)
f.cplx.angle(1 + 1j)      #c# Returns 0.7854 (pi/4)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Complex Conjugate Function}
Returns the complex conjugate: $\overline{a + bi} = a - bi$. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.cplx.conj", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real} (self-conjugate), \texttt{complex}.
\item Result: same type as input. Real numbers are self-conjugate.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised for non-numeric arguments.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.cplx.conj(3 + 4j)   #c# Returns 3 - 4j
f.cplx.conj(-2j)      #c# Returns 2j
f.cplx.conj(5)        #c# Returns 5 (real numbers are self-conjugate)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Polar Conversion Function}
Converts a complex number to polar form, returning \texttt{[magnitude, angle]}. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.cplx.polar", "(", ( int | real | complex ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, \texttt{complex}.
\item Result: \texttt{list} of two \texttt{real} values: \texttt{[|z|, arg(z)]}.
\item The angle is in radians, in the range $(-\pi, \pi]$.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised for non-numeric arguments.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.cplx.polar(3 + 4j)     #c# Returns [5.0, 0.9273]
f.cplx.polar(1 + 0j)     #c# Returns [1.0, 0.0]
f.cplx.polar(0 + 1j)     #c# Returns [1.0, 1.5708]
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Rectangular Conversion Function}
Creates a complex number from polar form (magnitude and angle in radians). \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.cplx.rect", "(", real, ",", real, ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: magnitude (\texttt{int} or \texttt{real}), angle in radians (\texttt{int} or \texttt{real}).
\item Result: \texttt{complex}.
\item Computes $z = r(\cos\theta + i\sin\theta)$.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if arguments are not numeric.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.cplx.rect(5, 0.9273)      #c# Returns approximately 3 + 4j
f.cplx.rect(1, 0)           #c# Returns 1 + 0j
f.cplx.rect(1, 1.5708)      #c# Returns approximately 0 + 1j
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Cis Function}
Computes the cis function: $\text{cis}(\theta) = \cos(\theta) + i \cdot \sin(\theta) = e^{i\theta}$. Returns a complex number on the unit circle at angle $\theta$ (radians). \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.cplx.cis", "(", ( int | real ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: angle in radians (\texttt{int} or \texttt{real}).
\item Result: \texttt{complex}.
\item Computes $\text{cis}(\theta) = \cos(\theta) + i \cdot \sin(\theta)$.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.cplx.cis(0)           #c# Returns 1 + 0j
f.cplx.cis(1.5708)      #c# Returns approximately 0 + 1j  (pi/2)
f.cplx.cis(3.14159)     #c# Returns approximately -1 + 0j (pi)
f.cplx.cis(-1.5708)     #c# Returns approximately 0 - 1j  (-pi/2)
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Radians to Degrees Function}
Converts an angle from radians to degrees. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.rad2deg", "(", ( int | real ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\item Result: \texttt{real}.
\item Computes $\text{degrees} = \text{radians} \times \frac{180}{\pi}$.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.rad2deg(3.14159)   #c# Returns 180.0
f.trig.rad2deg(1.5708)    #c# Returns 90.0
f.trig.rad2deg(0)         #c# Returns 0.0
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Degrees to Radians Function}
Converts an angle from degrees to radians. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.trig.deg2rad", "(", ( int | real ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\item Result: \texttt{real}.
\item Computes $\text{radians} = \text{degrees} \times \frac{\pi}{180}$.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.trig.deg2rad(180)   #c# Returns 3.14159
f.trig.deg2rad(90)    #c# Returns 1.5708
f.trig.deg2rad(0)     #c# Returns 0.0
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Floor Function}
Rounds the argument down to the nearest integer. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.floor", "(", ( int | real ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int} (returned as-is), \texttt{real}.
\item Result: \texttt{int}.
\item Returns the largest integer $\leq$ the argument.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.floor(2.7)   #c# Returns 2
f.math.floor(-1.3)  #c# Returns -2
f.math.floor(5)     #c# Returns 5
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Ceiling Function}
Rounds the argument up to the nearest integer. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.ceil", "(", ( int | real ), ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int} (returned as-is), \texttt{real}.
\item Result: \texttt{int}.
\item Returns the smallest integer $\geq$ the argument.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.ceil(2.3)    #c# Returns 3
f.math.ceil(-2.7)   #c# Returns -2
f.math.ceil(5)      #c# Returns 5
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Round Function}
Rounds the argument to the nearest value. Without \texttt{digits}, rounds to the nearest integer. With \texttt{digits}, rounds to the specified number of decimal places. Negative \texttt{digits} rounds to the nearest $10^n$: \texttt{digits=-1} rounds to nearest 10, \texttt{digits=-2} to nearest 100, etc. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.round", "(", ( int | real | tensor ), [",", "digits", "=", int], ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: value (\texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, or \texttt{tensor}), optional \texttt{digits} (\texttt{int}, default 0).
\item For tensors: applies rounding element-wise, returns tensor of same shape.
\item Result: \texttt{int} when no \texttt{digits} specified and input is \texttt{int}; \texttt{int} when \texttt{digits} $\geq 0$; \texttt{real} when \texttt{digits} $< 0$.
\item Uses banker's rounding (round half to even).
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the first argument is not \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, or \texttt{tensor}.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if \texttt{digits} is not \texttt{int}.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.round(2.5)                        #c# Returns 2 (banker's rounding)
f.math.round(2.4)                        #c# Returns 2
f.math.round(3.14159, digits=2)          #c# Returns 3.14
f.math.round(3.14159, digits=0)          #c# Returns 3
f.math.round(3.14159, digits=-1)         #c# Returns 0
f.math.round(155, digits=-2)             #c# Returns 200
f.math.round(|[1.2, 2.7, 3.0])           #c# Returns |[1, 3, 3]
f.math.round(|[1.5, 2.5, 3.5])           #c# Returns |[2, 2, 4]
f.math.round(|[1.234, 5.678], digits=1)  #c# Returns |[1.2, 5.7]
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{E-Series Rounding Function}
Rounds a value to the nearest standard E-series value. The E-series are logarithmically spaced value series used for resistors, capacitors, and inductors. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.eseries", "(", ( int | real ), ",", string, [",", "rm", "=", string], ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: value (\texttt{int} or \texttt{real}), series name (\texttt{string}: \texttt{"E3"}, \texttt{"E6"}, \texttt{"E12"}, \texttt{"E24"}, \texttt{"E48"}, \texttt{"E96"}, or \texttt{"E192"}, case-insensitive), optional \texttt{rm} (\texttt{string}: \texttt{"nearest"} (default), \texttt{"floor"}, or \texttt{"ceil"}).
\item Result: \texttt{real} in the selected series, scaled to the correct decade.
\item Zero returns \texttt{0} directly ($0\Omega$ resistors are valid components).
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the first argument is not \texttt{int} or \texttt{real}.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the second argument is not a \texttt{string}.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if the value is negative.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if the series name is not one of the valid E-series.
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if the rounding mode is not one of \texttt{"nearest"}, \texttt{"floor"}, or \texttt{"ceil"}.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.eseries(330, "E24")              #c# Returns 330
f.math.eseries(350, "E24")              #c# Returns 330
f.math.eseries(350, "E12")              #c# Returns 330
f.math.eseries(350, "E6")               #c# Returns 330
f.math.eseries(390, "E24")              #c# Returns 390
f.math.eseries(4.7e3, "E12")            #c# Returns 4700
f.math.eseries(1.5e3, "E3")             #c# Returns 2200
f.math.eseries(330, "e24")              #c# Returns 330 (case-insensitive)
f.math.eseries(0, "E24")                #c# Returns 0
f.math.eseries(330, "E5")               #c# Value Error
f.math.eseries(-100, "E24")             #c# Value Error
f.math.eseries("abc", "E24")            #c# Type Error
f.math.eseries(330, "E24", rm="floor")  #c# Returns 330
f.math.eseries(350, "E24", rm="ceil")   #c# Returns 390
\end{verbatim}

\paragraph{E-Series Reference}
All values are base values within one decade (1.0 to 10). Multiply by $10^n$ for other decades. Every value in E($n$) is also a member of E($2n$).\\
\textbf{E3} (20\%): 1.0, 2.2, 4.7\\
\textbf{E6} (20\%): 1.0, 1.5, 2.2, 3.3, 4.7, 6.8\\
\textbf{E12} (10\%): 1.0, 1.2, 1.5, 1.8, 2.2, 2.7, 3.3, 3.9, 4.7, 5.6, 6.8, 8.2\\
\textbf{E24} (5\%): 1.0, 1.1, 1.2, 1.3, 1.5, 1.6, 1.8, 2.0, 2.2, 2.4, 2.7, 3.0, 3.3, 3.6, 3.9, 4.3, 4.7, 5.1, 5.6, 6.2, 6.8, 7.5, 8.2, 9.1\\
\textbf{E48} (2\%): 1.00, 1.05, 1.10, 1.15, 1.21, 1.27, 1.33, 1.40, 1.47, 1.54, 1.62, 1.69, 1.78, 1.87, 1.96, 2.05, 2.15, 2.26, 2.37, 2.49, 2.61, 2.74, 2.87, 3.01, 3.16, 3.32, 3.48, 3.65, 3.83, 4.02, 4.22, 4.42, 4.64, 4.87, 5.11, 5.36, 5.62, 5.90, 6.19, 6.49, 6.81, 7.15, 7.50, 7.87, 8.25, 8.66, 9.09, 9.53\\
\textbf{E96} (1\%): 1.00, 1.02, 1.05, 1.07, 1.10, 1.13, 1.15, 1.18, 1.21, 1.24, 1.27, 1.30, 1.33, 1.37, 1.40, 1.43, 1.47, 1.50, 1.54, 1.58, 1.62, 1.65, 1.69, 1.74, 1.78, 1.82, 1.87, 1.91, 1.96, 2.00, 2.05, 2.10, 2.15, 2.21, 2.26, 2.32, 2.37, 2.43, 2.49, 2.55, 2.61, 2.67, 2.74, 2.80, 2.87, 2.94, 3.01, 3.09, 3.16, 3.24, 3.32, 3.40, 3.48, 3.57, 3.65, 3.74, 3.83, 3.92, 4.02, 4.12, 4.22, 4.32, 4.42, 4.53, 4.64, 4.75, 4.87, 4.99, 5.11, 5.23, 5.36, 5.49, 5.62, 5.76, 5.90, 6.04, 6.19, 6.34, 6.49, 6.65, 6.81, 6.98, 7.15, 7.32, 7.50, 7.68, 7.87, 8.06, 8.25, 8.45, 8.66, 8.87, 9.09, 9.31, 9.53, 9.76\\
\textbf{E192} (0.1\%): 1.00, 1.01, 1.02, 1.04, 1.05, 1.06, 1.07, 1.09, 1.10, 1.11, 1.13, 1.14, 1.15, 1.17, 1.18, 1.20, 1.21, 1.23, 1.24, 1.26, 1.27, 1.29, 1.30, 1.32, 1.33, 1.35, 1.37, 1.38, 1.40, 1.42, 1.43, 1.45, 1.47, 1.49, 1.50, 1.52, 1.54, 1.56, 1.58, 1.60, 1.62, 1.64, 1.65, 1.67, 1.69, 1.72, 1.74, 1.76, 1.78, 1.80, 1.82, 1.84, 1.87, 1.89, 1.91, 1.93, 1.96, 1.98, 2.00, 2.03, 2.05, 2.08, 2.10, 2.13, 2.15, 2.18, 2.21, 2.23, 2.26, 2.29, 2.32, 2.34, 2.37, 2.40, 2.43, 2.46, 2.49, 2.52, 2.55, 2.58, 2.61, 2.64, 2.67, 2.71, 2.74, 2.77, 2.80, 2.84, 2.87, 2.91, 2.94, 2.98, 3.01, 3.05, 3.09, 3.12, 3.16, 3.20, 3.24, 3.28, 3.32, 3.36, 3.40, 3.44, 3.48, 3.52, 3.57, 3.61, 3.65, 3.70, 3.74, 3.79, 3.83, 3.88, 3.92, 3.97, 4.02, 4.07, 4.12, 4.17, 4.22, 4.27, 4.32, 4.37, 4.42, 4.48, 4.53, 4.59, 4.64, 4.70, 4.75, 4.81, 4.87, 4.93, 4.99, 5.05, 5.11, 5.17, 5.23, 5.30, 5.36, 5.42, 5.49, 5.56, 5.62, 5.69, 5.76, 5.83, 5.90, 5.97, 6.04, 6.12, 6.19, 6.26, 6.34, 6.42, 6.49, 6.57, 6.65, 6.73, 6.81, 6.90, 6.98, 7.06, 7.15, 7.23, 7.32, 7.41, 7.50, 7.59, 7.68, 7.77, 7.87, 7.96, 8.06, 8.16, 8.25, 8.35, 8.45, 8.56, 8.66, 8.76, 8.87, 8.98, 9.09, 9.20, 9.31, 9.42, 9.53, 9.65, 9.76, 9.88


\subsubsection{Minimum Function}
Returns the minimum value in a list. For tensors, an optional \texttt{axis} parameter reduces along that axis. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.min", "(", list, [ ",", "axis", "=", int ], [ ",", "ignoreNan", "=", bool ], ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{list} of comparable elements, or \texttt{tensor}. Optional \texttt{axis}: \texttt{int} (tensor only).
\item Result: the smallest element in the list/array.
\item For tensors with \texttt{axis}, reduces along the specified axis, returning an tensor.
\item Optional \texttt{ignoreNan}: if \texttt{true}, NaN elements are skipped (default: \texttt{false}).
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if the list is empty.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not a list or tensor.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if elements are not comparable (e.g., mixing \texttt{int} and \texttt{string}).
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.min([3, 1, 4, 1, 5])                     #c# Returns 1
f.math.min([[3,1],[4,2]], axis=0)               #c# Returns [3, 1]
f.math.min([[3,1],[4,2]], axis=1)               #c# Returns [1, 2]
f.math.min([3, c.math.NaN, 1], ignoreNan=true)  #c# Returns 1
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Maximum Function}
Returns the maximum value in a list. For tensors, an optional \texttt{axis} parameter reduces along that axis. \\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.math.max", "(", list, [ ",", "axis", "=", int ], [ ",", "ignoreNan", "=", bool ], ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{list} of comparable elements, or \texttt{tensor}. Optional \texttt{axis}: \texttt{int} (tensor only).
\item Result: the largest element in the list/array.
\item For tensors with \texttt{axis}, reduces along the specified axis, returning an tensor.
\item Optional \texttt{ignoreNan}: if \texttt{true}, NaN elements are skipped (default: \texttt{false}).
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if the list is empty.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if the argument is not a list or tensor.
\item A \texttt{Type Error} is raised if elements are not comparable.
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.math.max([3, 1, 4, 1, 5])                     #c# Returns 5
f.math.max([[3,1],[4,2]], axis=0)               #c# Returns [4, 2]
f.math.max([[3,1],[4,2]], axis=1)               #c# Returns [3, 4]
f.math.max([3, c.math.NaN, 1], ignoreNan=true)  #c# Returns 3
\end{verbatim}

\subsubsection{Element-wise Maximum}
The \texttt{emax} function returns the maximum of two operands element-wise.\\
Morphology:
\begin{verbatim}
EBNF:
"f.tnsr.emax", "(", argument, ",", argument, ")"
\end{verbatim}
\begin{itemize}
\item Operands: \texttt{int}, \texttt{real}, or \texttt{tensor}
\item If one operand is scalar, it is broadcast against the other
\item Element-wise: $\text{result}[i] = \max(a[i], b[i])$
\item NaN propagates: \texttt{emax(5, NaN)} returns \texttt{NaN}
\item A \texttt{Value Error} is raised if tensor shapes differ (except scalar broadcast).
\end{itemize}
Examples:
\begin{verbatim}
f.tnsr.emax(3, 5)                             #c# Returns 5
